Quantitative homogenization and hydrodynamic limit of non-gradient exclusion process
Tadahisa Funaki, Chenlin Gu, Han Wang

Abstract
. For the non-gradient exclusion process, we prove the quantitative homogenization in the approximation of the diffusion matrix and the conductivity by local functions. The proof relies on the renormalization approach developed by Armstrong, Kuusi, Mourrat, and Smart, while the new challenge here is the hard core constraint of particle number on every site. Therefore, a coarse-grained method is proposed to lift the configuration to a larger space without exclusion, and a gradient coupling between two systems is applied to capture the spatial cancellation. We then strengthen the convergence rate to be uniform concerning the density and integrate it into the work by Funaki, Uchiyama, and Yau [ IMA Vol. Math. Appl. , 77 (1996), pp. 1-40.] to yield a quantitative hydrodynamic limit. Our new approach avoids showing the characterization of closed forms and provides stronger results. The extension is discussed for the model in the presence of disorder on the bonds. MSC 2010: 82C22, 35B27, 60K35. Keywords: interacting particle system, non-gradient exclusion process, Kawasaki dynamics, diffusion matrix, quantitative homogenization, hydrodynamic limit.



1.
Introduction

The diffusion matrix
plays an important role in the study of the large-scale behaviors of
interacting particle systems. Among these systems, some are classified as
gradient model
if the current of the conserved quantity can be written as a sum of the difference between a local function and its spatial shift, and the others are called
non-gradient model
. Unlike the gradient model, the non-gradient model usually requires more techniques to derive the hydrodynamic limit, because the scaling yields a diverging factor and, moreover, its diffusion matrix does not have an explicit expression and one needs to add a correction. This idea goes back to the seminal work
[
74
]
of Varadhan, who studied the Ginzburg–Landau model. Later, the hydrodynamic limit was proved in several classical particle systems of non-gradient type:
the generalized symmetric exclusion process (GSEP)

[
52
]
by Kipnis, Landim and Olla;
the lattice gas

[
34
]
, also known as
the non-gradient exclusion process
or
speed-change Kawasaki dynamics
, by Funaki, Uchiyama and Yau, and the general lattice gas with mixing condition
[
75
]
by Varadhan and Yau;
the multi-type simple symmetric exclusion process (multi-type SSEP)

[
69
]
by Quastel, etc. The equilibrium fluctuation in non-gradient models was studied later in
[
31
,
57
,
19
]
, and the regularity of the diffusion matrix was discussed in a series of work
[
54
,
65
,
66
,
67
,
16
]
. One can also refer
[
72
,
51
]
for the basic background and
[
71
]
for the relation between the gradient condition and the Green–Kubo formula.

It is natural to ask the convergence rate, and the quantitative hydrodynamic limit has received attentions recently. The related results can be found for the Ginzburg–Landau model in
[
26
,
27
]
, which was developed on
[
45
]
. Very recently,
[
60
]
proposed a
consistence-stability approach
to obtain the quantitative hydrodynamic limit in Wasserstein-1 distance for several models including the zero-range process, the Ginzburg–Landau model, and the simple exclusion process (see
[
59
, Chapter 5.5]
). However, all these results are for the gradient model, and there is no results for the non-gradient model in the literature to the best of our knowledge. This is because, as mentioned in the last paragraph, a diverging factor appears and the diffusion matrix is more complicated in the non-gradient model. Such obstacle was already observed in the proof in
[
34
,
31
]
, where several key error terms are finally reduced to the approximation of the diffusion matrix or
the conductivity
; see
[
34
, (2.5) and Section 5]
. These two fundamental quantities are defined using variational formulas and are related by the Einstein relation. Therefore, both of them can be approximated qualitatively by a sequence of local functions, but the convergence rate is unknown.

In this paper, we answer the question above by a concrete construction of desired local functions. As Varadhan observed the link between the interacting systems and homogenization, in the sense of averaging and gradient replacement which kills the diverging factor, in the earlier work
[
74
]
, the new improvement comes from the recent progress in the quantitative homogenization theory; see
[
15
,
14
,
9
,
10
,
6
,
5
,
11
,
7
]
based on the renormalization approach, and
[
64
,
62
,
39
,
40
,
37
,
41
,
38
]
based on another approach using spectral inequalities. As an example, for the
\nabla\phi
interface model studied in
[
33
]
, a quantitative hydrodynamic limit is obtained in
[
4
]
using the renormalization approach. Also inspired by the renormalization approach,
[
35
]
studies a similar diffusion matrix problem in continuous configuration space, and a quantitative equilibrium fluctuation is obtained recently in
[
47
]
under the same setting. These work pave the way for the quantitative homogenization theory in interacting particle systems, but the continuous configuration model there relaxes the hard core constraint by allowing arbitrarily large number of particles in the unit volume. As a consequence, to apply the existing results to a lattice particle model of exclusion rule still meets technical challenges in math. The present paper aims to resolve these difficulties and is the first example to establish quantitative homogenization theory on the non-gradient exclusion process. Our main result not only generalizes
[
35
]
to the non-gradient exclusion process, but also improves in the sense that we construct
one
local corrector to realize uniform convergence of conductivity for
every
particle density. Moreover, this density-uniform homogenization can be integrated into the relative entropy method in the classical work
[
34
]
, and then establishes a quantitative hydrodynamic limit; namely, our result provides the convergence rate in the hydrodynamic limit for non-gradient exclusion process. We emphasize that our method is new and, in particular, avoids to prove the characterization of closed forms, which is usually required and useful to show the hydrodynamic limit for non-gradient models in qualitative level, but not sufficient to obtain the convergence rate as in this paper.

Our proof is also robust. Viewing the recent interests on the exclusion process in random environment (see
[
70
,
49
,
50
,
42
,
30
,
43
,
28
,
29
]
), we give a quick generalization for our case when the external disorder is posed on the bonds. We believe the results in this paper can be extended to other models including GSEP and multi-type SSEP in the future work.

1.1.
Main results

In this part, we state our main results.

We recall quickly the necessary notations of the exclusion process and the results in the previous work. Let
{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be the Euclidean lattice and we use
\mathcal{X}:=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}
to stand for the space of the configuration of particles under exclusion rule. The element of
\mathcal{X}
will be denoted by
\eta=\{\eta_{x}:x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\}
. Here
\eta_{x}=0
means the site
x
is vacant and
\eta_{x}=1
means the site is occupied by one particle. We denote by
y\sim x
for
x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
if
|x-y|=1
. Then
\{x,y\}
is called an (undirected) bond. For every
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we denote by
\Lambda^{*}
the bond in
\Lambda
that

(1.1)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{*}:=\{\{x,y\}:x,y\in\Lambda,x\sim y\}.

For
x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, the exchange operator
\eta^{x,y}
is defined as

\displaystyle(\eta^{x,y})_{z}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\eta_{z},&\qquad z\neq x,y;\\
\eta_{y},&\qquad z=x;\\
\eta_{x},&\qquad z=y.\end{array}\right.

Especially, when
b=\{x,y\}
is a bond, we also write
\eta^{b}
instead of
\eta^{x,y}
, and define the Kawasaki operator
\pi_{b}\equiv\pi_{x,y}

(1.5)

\displaystyle\pi_{b}f(\eta):=f(\eta^{b})-f(\eta).

For any
x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, the translation operator
\tau_{x}
is defined as

(1.6)

\displaystyle(\tau_{x}\eta)_{y}:=\eta_{x+y},

and for function
f
on
\mathcal{X}
, we also define
\tau_{x}f
as

(1.7)

\displaystyle(\tau_{x}f)(\eta)=f(\tau_{x}\eta).

The
non-gradient exclusion process
on
{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is defined by the generator below

(1.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}:=\sum_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}c_{b}(\eta)\pi_{b}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}:|x-y|=1}c_{x,y}(\eta)\pi_{x,y},

where the family of functions

(1.9)

\displaystyle\{c_{b}(\eta)\equiv c_{x,y}(\eta)=c_{y,x}(\eta);b=\{x,y\}\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}\},

determine the jump rate of particles on the nearest bonds. This model is also called
the speed-change Kawasaki dynamics
or
the lattice gas
in the literature, and we will also use these names alternatively from time to time in the paper.

We suppose the following conditions for the jump rate throughout the paper without specific explanation.

Hypothesis 1.1
.

The following conditions are supposed for
\{c_{b}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}
.

(1)

Non-degenerate and local:
c_{x,y}(\eta)
depends only on
\{\eta_{z}:|z-x|\leqslant\mathbf{r}\}
for some integer
\mathbf{r}>0
, and is bounded on two sides
1\leqslant c_{x,y}(\eta)\leqslant\lambda
.

(2)

Spatially homogeneous: for all
\{x,y\}\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}
,
c_{x,y}=\tau_{x}c_{0,y-x}
.

(3)

Detailed balance under Bernoulli measures:
c_{x,y}(\eta)
is independent of
\{\eta_{x},\eta_{y}\}
.

This model is known of non-gradient type, i.e. we cannot find functions
\{h_{i,j}\}_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant d}
such that
c_{0,e_{i}}(\eta)(\eta_{e_{i}}-\eta_{0})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left((\tau_{e_{j}}h_{i,j})(\eta)-h_{i,j}(\eta)\right)
for general
\{c_{b}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}
, with
\{e_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant d}
the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.

The hydrodynamic limit of this speed-change Kawasaki dynamics on torus is proved in
[
34
]
. Let
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}:=(\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})^{d}
be the lattice torus of scale
N
, where we can define all the notations by replacing
{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
with
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}
. We denote by
\mathcal{X}_{N}:=\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}}
the configuration space on
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}
, and define
\eta^{N}(t)=\{\eta^{N}_{x}(t),x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}\}
as the
\mathcal{X}_{N}
-valued Markov jump process on torus governed by the generator
\mathcal{L}_{N}:=N^{2}\mathcal{L}
, the counterpart of (
1.8
) on
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}
. The macroscopic empirical measure of
\eta^{N}(t)
is defined as

(1.10)

\displaystyle\rho^{N}(t,{\mathrm{d}}v):=N^{-d}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}}\eta^{N}_{x}(t)\delta_{x/N}({\mathrm{d}}v),\qquad v\in\mathbb{T}^{d},

and the limit is the solution of a nonlinear diffusion equation

(1.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho(t,v)=\nabla\cdot(D(\rho(t,v))\nabla\rho(t,v)),\qquad(t,v)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{T}^{d}.

Here
\mathbb{T}^{d}={\mathbb{R}^{d}}/{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is the continuous torus and
D:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is
the diffusion matrix
defined by
the Einstein relation

(1.12)

\displaystyle D(\rho):=\frac{\mathbf{c}(\rho)}{2\chi(\rho)},

where
\chi(\rho)
is
the compressibility

(1.13)

\displaystyle\chi(\rho):=\rho(1-\rho),

and
\mathbf{c}(\rho)
is
the effective conductivity
defined as follows. We construct at first a quadratic form with respect to the function
F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}

(1.14)

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)\xi=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|x|=1}\left\langle{c_{0,x}\left(\xi\cdot\left\{x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})-\pi_{0,x}(\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{y}F)\right\}\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho},

where
\mathcal{F}_{0}
is the local function space on
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}:=(\mathcal{F}_{0})^{d}
, and
\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho}
stands for the expectation under Bernoulli product measure of density
\rho\in[0,1]
. Then
\mathbf{c}(\rho)
is the minimization of
\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)

(1.15)

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho)\xi:=\inf_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}}\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)\xi.

Under the assumption that (
1.11
) has a smooth initial density
\rho_{0}=\rho_{0}(v)
and
\eta^{N}(0)
is close to the local equilibrium with density profile
\rho_{0}(v)
in the sense of relative entropy
h_{N}(0)=o(1)
(see (
7.13
) and the beginning of the proof of Thoerem
1.3
for the definition),
[
34
, Theorem 1.1]
proves that for every
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and
\varepsilon>0

(1.16)

\displaystyle P\left[\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi(v)\rho^{N}(t,{\mathrm{d}}v)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi(v)\rho(t,{\mathrm{d}}v)\right|>\varepsilon\right]\xrightarrow{N\to\infty}0.

Here
P
stands for the probability space of the process
(\eta^{N}(t))_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}
, and
\rho(t,{\mathrm{d}}v):=\rho(t,v){\mathrm{d}}v
.

The proof of hydrodynamic limit in
[
34
]
relies on
the relative entropy method
. One key step is to prove that, for every
\beta>0
and small
\delta>0
, we have the following estimate for the normalized relative entropy (with some other terms omitting details on the right-hand side)

(1.17)

\displaystyle h_{N}(t)\leqslant h_{N}(0)+\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{t}h_{N}(s)\,{\mathrm{d}}s+C(\beta+1)\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F)|+\frac{C}{\beta}+Q_{N}.

Here the quantity
R(\rho;F)
comes from the conductivity (
1.14
) and (
1.15
), which is defined as

(1.18)

\displaystyle R(\rho;F):=\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)-\mathbf{c}(\rho),

see (
7.16
) for the other error terms in
Q_{N}
. To conclude (
1.16
), we take a large
\beta
such that the right-hand side of (
1.17
) is small, and then apply Gronwall’s inequality and the entropy inequality. Therefore, we also need the decay from the term
R(\rho;F)
, which was proved in
[
34
, Lemma 2.1]
that

(1.19)

\displaystyle\inf_{F\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}}\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F)|=0.

The object of this paper is to give a convergence rate of (
1.16
). As explained briefly above, this is finally reduced to
R(\rho;F)
and we need to study (
1.19
) more precisely. It can be considered as a quantitative homogenization of the fundamental quantity
\mathbf{c}(\rho)
, which is our main result stated as follows. In the statement,
\Lambda_{L}:=(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{d}\cap{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
stands for a hypercube of side length around
L\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}(\Lambda_{L})
is the subset of
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}
which contains
\sigma(\{\eta_{x}\}_{x\in\Lambda_{L}})
-measurable local functions.

Theorem 1.2
.

Under Hypothesis
1.1
, there exists an exponent
\gamma(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})>0
and a positive constant
C(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})<\infty
, such that

(1.20)

\displaystyle\inf_{F_{L}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})}\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F_{L})|\leqslant CL^{-\gamma}.

We will also give the concrete construction of the local function achieving the estimate above in our proof; see Section
6.4
for details. Then as expected, we can insert the estimate in (
1.17
), and obtain a quantitative hydrodynamic limit after careful investigation.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
\rho(t,v)
be the solution of the hydrodynamic
equation (
1.11
) for
t\in[0,T]
with a smooth initial value

\rho_{0}
such that
0<\rho_{0}(v)<1
. Assume that
f_{0}
and
\psi_{0}
defined in (
7.14
)
satisfy the entropy condition
h_{N}(0)\leqslant CN^{-\alpha}
for some

C,\alpha>0
. Then, for every
\varepsilon,T>0
and
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, there exists
\kappa>0
and
C=C(\varepsilon,T,\phi)>0
such that

(1.21)

\displaystyle P\left[\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi(v)\rho^{N}(t,{\mathrm{d}}v)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi(v)\rho(t,{\mathrm{d}}v)\right|>\varepsilon\right]\leqslant CN^{-\kappa},

holds for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Our proof relies on the homogenization theory, so let us also state a variant of (
1.20
), which is an intermediate step but is related to the CLT variance estimate in
[
34
, Section 5]
. Consider a formal sum
\ell_{\xi}=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}(\xi\cdot x)\eta_{x}
, then we notice that the term
\xi\cdot x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})
in (
1.14
) is

\displaystyle\xi\cdot x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})=-\pi_{0,x}\ell_{\xi}.

Thus (
1.15
) is the minimization of the Dirichlet energy of a linear statistic plus some correction term. Inspired from the ergodic theory, a natural finite-volume approximation in
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
of (
1.15
) should be

(1.22)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)\xi:=\inf_{v\in\ell_{\xi}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\left\langle{v(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}v)}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

Here
\Lambda^{-}
is the interior of
\Lambda
and
\overline{\Lambda^{*}}
is the set of bonds issued from
\Lambda
(see (
1.36
), (
1.37
) for details).
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})
is the set of
\sigma(\{\eta_{x}\}_{x\in\Lambda^{-}})
-measurable local functions, and
\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}
is the generator on
\Lambda

(1.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}v:=\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}c_{b}\pi_{b}v.

A well-known integration by part formula under
\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho}
also tells us

(1.24)

\displaystyle\left\langle{v(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}v)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho},

so (
1.22
) can be interpreted as the minimization of the Dirichlet energy contributed by every particle. We expect that
\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)
converges to
\mathbf{c}(\rho)
when
\Lambda\nearrow{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.

A similar definition like (
1.22
) can be also posed under the canonical ensemble

(1.25)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\hat{\mathbf{c}}(\Lambda,N)\xi:=\inf_{v\in\ell_{\xi}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\left\langle{v(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}v)}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N},

where
\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}
is the expectation under the uniform measure of
N
particles in
\Lambda
. Notice that the quantity
\hat{\mathbf{c}}(\Lambda,N)
still depends on the configuration outside
\Lambda
. On the other hand, because the jump rate
c
is of finite range
\mathbf{r}
by Hypothesis
1.1
, the influence from the boundary layer vanishes when
\Lambda\nearrow{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
. Thus
\hat{\mathbf{c}}(\Lambda,N)
should be close to
\mathbf{c}(N/|\Lambda|)
in large scale. We prove the convergence of these two quantities in the following theorem.

Theorem 1.4
.

Under Hypothesis
1.1
, there exists a constant
C(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})<\infty
and two exponents
{\gamma_{1}(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r}),\gamma_{2}(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})>0}
such that for every
L,M\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

(1.26)

\displaystyle\left|\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda_{L})-\mathbf{c}(\rho)\right|\leqslant CL^{-\gamma_{1}},

and

(1.27)

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\mathbf{c}}(\Lambda_{L},M)-\mathbf{c}(M/|\Lambda_{L}|)\right|\leqslant CL^{-\gamma_{2}}.

Recalling the Einstein relation (
1.12
), the results above also imply the convergence rate of the diffusion matrix
D(\rho)
, for all
\rho\in(0,1)
, by local functions or finite-volume approximation.

We also obtain an estimate similar to (
1.26
) when the disorder is posed on bonds. To lighten the notation, we leave the related discussion in Section
8
.

Remark 1.5
.

The choice of notation
\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}
here is just for the technical convenience and the consistence. Lemma
A.1
ensures the stability of Theorem
1.4
in the general domain, so we can replace
\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}
in (
1.23
) by the canonical notation
\Lambda^{*}
defined in (
1.1
) and the statement still holds.

1.2.
Strategy of the proof

The idea of the proof in this paper is inspired by recent developments in the quantitative homogenization, and in particular on the renormalization approach developed in
[
15
,
14
,
9
,
10
,
6
,
5
]
; see monographs
[
11
,
7
]
and
[
63
]
for a gentle introduction. This renormalization approach has shown its robustness in a number of other settings including the parabolic equations
[
1
]
, finite-difference equations on percolation clusters
[
3
,
21
,
23
]
, differential forms
[
22
]
, the “
\nabla\phi
” interface model
[
20
,
12
,
13
,
4
]
, and the Villain model
[
24
]
. Recently
[
35
,
36
,
47
]
also generalizes the theory to an interacting particle system in continuous space
without
exclusion, thus let us discuss the novelty and contribution in this paper.

1.2.1.
Renormalization with coarse-grained lifting

We employ the renormalization approach to prove (
1.26
) of Theorem
1.4
, which serves as the cornerstone for the other results. The ingredients of the renormalization approach can be roughly divided into two parts:

(1)

Find the subadditive quantity for the desired limit, and use the gap between the subadditive quantity and its dual quantity to control the convergence rate.

(2)

Establish various analytic tools, where the two key estimates are

•

the Caccioppoli inequality;

•

the multiscale Poincaré inequality.

The first part is more conceptual, and the quantity
\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)
defined in (
1.22
) is a good candidate satisfying the subadditivity in our setting. That is, for disjoint union
\Lambda=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}\Lambda^{(i)}
, we have

(1.28)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{|\Lambda^{(i)}|}{|\Lambda|}\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda^{(i)}).

The main issue comes from the second part. The two key inequalities are well-developed for the elliptic equation on
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, but can become challenging in other settings. We should also highlight that, the two inequalities are more than the technical estimates, but the essentials of quantitative homogenization, because they characterize the elliptic conditions in the large scale; see the recent work for the homogenization in high contrast
[
2
]
,
[
8
]
. In the exclusion process, these inequalities are not accessible directly, and we need to relax them respectively to
the modified Caccioppoli inequality
and
the weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality
, which are explained in the following paragraphs more carefully.

The classical Caccioppoli inequality describes the inner regularity of the elliptic equations, but seems missing in the particle systems due to the influence of particles near the boundary. Therefore, the modified Caccioppoli inequality is developed in the previous work
[
35
, Proposition 3.9]
, which differs from the classical one, but captures the same spirit. In the present work, its counterpart in the exclusion process is also recovered in Proposition
2.6
. The proof requires more work due to the microscopic behavior; see Lemma
2.1
and Lemma
2.7
. As new inputs, the Glauber derivative and
the reverse Efron–Stein inequality
are also involved.

The multiscale Poincaré inequality (see
[
11
, Proposition 1.12 and Corollary 1.14]
for example) improves the estimate of the classical Poincaré inequality when the function has the spatial cancellation property, which is the case in homogenization. It meets obstacles to derive the counterpart for Kawasaki dynamics, because the generator is not smooth enough to ensure the
H^{2}
estimate needed in the proof. Actually, even the generator of the simple symmetric exclusion shows interaction in higher order derivative; see Remark
2.10
for details. For this reason, we believe the multiscale Poincaré inequality should live in the homogenized particle system
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{N}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}
where
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,3,\cdots\}
, i.e. the independent particles, and we prove it in Section
2.2
.

Then a crucial problem is how to apply an inequality on
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{N}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}
to the functions on
\mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}
. Similar problem on the percolation setting was also posed, and a possible solution is the coarse-grained strategy; see
[
3
,
21
,
46
,
23
]
. We hope to implement this idea in the exclusion processes: for every function
u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, we aim to find a coarsened function
[u]:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
on the larger space such that for every
\eta\in\mathcal{X}
as the grain of
\widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, it satisfies

(1.29)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}\simeq\eta\Longrightarrow[u](\widetilde{\eta})\simeq u(\eta).

A naive candidate of grain
\eta
is the one close to
\widetilde{\eta}
under some distance. However, different from the Bernoulli percolation setting, in particle systems the space of grain
\mathcal{X}
is a very sparse subset of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, so we face
the curse of dimension
which may extremely enlarge the error in (
1.29
). This is also the key difficulty compared to the previous work
[
35
]
. Our solution turns out not only a coarse-grained method of functions, but also a
lift
from
\mathcal{X}
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, i.e. we can represent the function on Kawasaki dynamics using a coupled independent particles. More precisely, for every
\widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, we set its grain
[\widetilde{\eta}]\in\mathcal{X}
as

\displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\qquad[\widetilde{\eta}]_{x}:=\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\geqslant 1\right\}},

and the coarsened function for
u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
as

\displaystyle[u]:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad[u](\widetilde{\eta}):=u([\widetilde{\eta}]).

This
coarse-grained lifting
is introduced in Section
3
. Based on this technique, we also obtain a
gradient coupling
between two systems (see Propositions
3.2
and
3.6
), and a weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality on Kawasaki dynamics. They are the main tools to evaluate the flatness of the functions in Proposition
5.3
.

Figure 1.
An illustration for the coarse-grained lifting between the Kawasaki dynamics and independent particles.

1.2.2.
Regularity and uniform convergence

Usually the convergence rate depends on the particle density, so let us explain why a uniform convergence is valid. A first qualitative argument is that, our finite-volume approximation decreases to the limit, and the limit function
\rho\mapsto\mathbf{c}(\rho)
is continuous thanks to
[
65
]
, so Dini’s theorem applies and the convergence is uniform. At the quantitative level, we highlight that, the only step where the density involves in analysis is the weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality, where some large factors can be added for the low density cases. Meanwhile, the modified Caccioppoli inequality (
2.10
) uses the elliptic regularity and the variance decay estimate (
5.20
) uses the spatial independence, so they are free from particle density. Here we notice that
\mathbf{c}
has a trivial bound by
\chi(\rho)=\rho(1-\rho)
, and this can help us at two endpoints.

We still need some more ingredients to pass the results from Theorem
1.4
to Theorem
1.2
. The uniform estimates in (
1.26
) can be seen as a weak version

\displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\inf_{F_{\rho,L}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})}R(\rho;F_{\rho,L})\leqslant CL^{-\gamma_{1}},

while quantity in (
1.20
) is a strong version, and usually we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\inf_{F_{\rho,L}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})}R(\rho;F_{\rho,L})\leqslant\inf_{F_{L}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})}\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}R(\rho;F_{L}).

We do not know whether there exists any duality property in the function
R(\rho;F)
, thus we make the proof by a direct construction. The minimizer
F
in this variational problem is actually
the correctors
in homogenization theory. In the renormalization step, we already get a candidate
\phi_{\rho,\Lambda,\xi}
for the problem (
1.22
), but it has dependence on the density. Our main task is to remove this dependence, so we propose a modified version of the local corrector: for disjoint union
\Lambda=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{N}\Lambda^{(i)}
, we make a combination of the local correctors on every subset

(1.30)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}_{\hat{\rho},\Lambda,\xi}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{\hat{\rho},\Lambda_{i},\xi},

where
\hat{\rho}
is an empirical density on the domain
\Lambda
instead of a fixed designed density. Insert this function in the problem (
1.22
), we obtain a uniform convergence under grand canonical ensemble by the following reasons.

(1)

The homogenization appears in the large scale, so (
1.28
) is nearly an equality and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{\rho,\Lambda_{i},\xi}
nearly equals
\phi_{\rho,\Lambda,\xi}
.

(2)

Given an empirical density
\hat{\rho}
, each local corrector lives as if under the grand canonical ensemble thanks to the local equivalence of ensembles. This is also the trick in the proof of (
1.27
).

(3)

The empirical density
\hat{\rho}
may also fluctuate when applying the Kawasaki operator, but this can be handled. On the one hand, we have the regularity of the mapping
\rho\mapsto\phi_{\rho,\Lambda_{i},\xi}
, and each fluctuation of density is just
1/|\Lambda|
. On the other hand, such fluctuation only happens on the boundary layer of
\Lambda
, whose order is dominated by the volume order in (
1.22
).

Similar argument actually has already appeared in the proof of (
1.19
) in
[
34
, Lemma 2.1]
. Besides the quantitative homogenization in (1), we also need to calculate carefully the errors in (2) and (3), i.e. that from local equivalence of ensembles and the regularity of density. They are discussed in detail in Sections
6.2
and
6.1
, and then we justify the density-free corrector (
1.30
) in Section
6.3
.

1.2.3.
Hydrodynamic Limit

Overall, our method well fits proving the hydrodynamic limit for the non-gradient exclusion process. The main difficulty to study non-gradient models lies, in general, in the fact that the microscopic current does not have a gradient form and this yields a diverging factor under the scaling. To overcome this difficulty, we need to show that, under a large space-time domain, one can replace such term by a well-behaving function of gradient form asymptotically. This is called
the gradient replacement
(see
[
34
, Theorem 3.2 and Lemma 3.4]
). For this, we usually need to show Varadhan’s lemma (see
[
74
, Theorem 5.2]
and
[
34
, Theorem 4.1]
) which gives the characterization of closed forms defined on a configuration space. We observe a connection between the gradient replacement and the dual quantity employed in the renormalization approach (see Section
7.1
), thus our method provides another new route for the non-gradient hydrodynamic limit avoiding Varadhan’s lemma; in fact, stronger in the sense that we obtain a quantitative convergence rate.

1.3.
Organization of paper

The rest of paper is organized as follows. We finish the introduction with a summary of notations, especially those about function spaces. In Section
2
, we present the necessary tools including the modified Caccioppoli inequality for the Kawasaki dynamics. Then we introduce the coarse-grained lifting technique in Section
3
, and use it to derive the weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality. Sections
4
and
5
are devoted to the convergence rate, where we make use of the renormalization approach. Afterwards, we study the regularity of the local corrector and remove the density dependence in Section
6
. The quantitative hydrodynamic limit is proved in Section
7
and the extension for the disordered cases is discussed in Section
8
. See the outline in Figure
2
for details.

1.4.
Notations

We summarize the notations used throughout the paper.

1.4.1.
Geometry

We use
|\cdot|
to stand for the usual
\ell^{2}
-norm for the finite dimensional vector or matrices. Meanwhile, for any
x,y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we define

(1.31)

\displaystyle\dist(x,y):=\max\{|x_{1}-y_{1}|,|x_{2}-y_{2}|,\cdots,|x_{d}-y_{d}|\}.

This also generalizes to
\dist(x,\Lambda):=\inf_{y\in\Lambda}\dist(x,y)
for every subset
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
.

We denote by
\Lambda_{L}:=(\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{d}\cap{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
the hypercube of side length around
L
, where
L\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is not necessarily an integer for the flexibility. For every
m\in\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
, we also denote by
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}:=\left(-\frac{3^{m}}{2},\frac{3^{m}}{2}\right)^{d}\cap{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
the hypercube of side length
3^{m}
. For any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n<m
, we denote by
\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}:=3^{n}{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\cap{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{n}:=3^{n}{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
. Then using
\sqcup
as the disjoint union, we have the following partition

(1.32)

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}=\bigsqcup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}),

which provides convenience to implement the renormalization.

For any finite set
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we denote by
|\Lambda|
the number of vertices

(1.33)

\displaystyle|\Lambda|:=\#\{x:x\in\Lambda\},

and define the diameter as

(1.34)

\displaystyle\diam(\Lambda):=\max\{|x-y|:x,y\in\Lambda\}.

We also define
\partial\Lambda
as the boundary set of
\Lambda
that

(1.35)

\displaystyle\partial\Lambda:=\{x\in\Lambda:\exists y\notin\Lambda,y\sim x\},

and denote by
\Lambda^{-}
the interior of
\Lambda

(1.36)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{-}:=\Lambda\setminus\partial\Lambda.

Recall that the set of bonds of
\Lambda
is defined as
\Lambda^{*}
in (
1.1
). We define its enlarged version

(1.37)

\displaystyle\overline{\Lambda^{*}}:=\{\{x,y\}:x\in\Lambda,y=x+e_{i},i=1,2,\cdots,d\},

where
e_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is the
i
-th directed unit vector, and also denote by
\Lambda^{+}
the vertices concerned in (
1.37
)

(1.38)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{+}:=\Lambda\cup\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}(\Lambda+e_{i}).

One motivation is that, for
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
n<m
, despite of (
1.32
), we observe that
\bigcup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n})^{*}\subsetneq({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{*}
. On the other hand, (
1.37
) provides a better partition structure for bonds

(1.39)

\displaystyle\overline{({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})^{*}}=\bigsqcup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n}}\overline{(z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n})^{*}}.

For disjoint sets
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\Lambda\cap\Lambda^{\prime}=\emptyset
, we define
(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})^{*}
as the set of bonds between
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\prime}

(1.40)

\displaystyle(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})^{*}:=({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda^{*}\sqcup(\Lambda^{\prime})^{*})

Especially,
(\Lambda,\Lambda^{c})^{*}
is the set of bonds connecting
\Lambda
and its complement.

1.4.2.
Probability spaces

For every
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we denote by
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the
(\eta_{x})_{x\in\Lambda}
and we write
\mathscr{F}
short for
\mathscr{F}_{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}
. Given
\rho\in(0,1)
as the density of particle, and make use of
\mathbb{P}_{\rho}
as the Bernoulli product measure
\operatorname{Ber}(\rho)^{\otimes{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}
on
\mathcal{X}
, thus
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P}_{\rho})
is the triplet of probability space most used in this paper. For the expectation under
\mathbb{P}_{\rho}
, we use the notation
\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho}
or
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\cdot]
. We make use of
\mathbb{P}_{\rho,\Lambda},\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho,\Lambda}
when we restrict our measure on
(\eta_{x})_{x\in\Lambda}
. We also denote by
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,N,\zeta}
and
\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N,\zeta}
for the probability and expectation under the canonical ensemble, i.e.
N
particles distributed uniformly on different sites of
\Lambda
with the configuration
\zeta
on
\Lambda^{c}
. We usually omit
\zeta
and just write them as
\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,N}
and
\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}
.

1.4.3.
Function spaces

For every
1\leqslant p\leqslant\infty
, we denote by
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{L^{p}}
or
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{p}
the
L^{p}
norm over the probability space
(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P}_{\rho})
, and denote by
L^{p}(\mathcal{X},\mathscr{F},\mathbb{P}_{\rho})
or shortly
L^{p}
the set of random variables with finite norm. For any
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, let
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda)
be the set of
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}
-measurable local functions. We also define the Sobolev norm
H^{1}(\Lambda)
that

(1.41)

\displaystyle\left\|{f}\right\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\Lambda)}=\left\langle{f^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

For every local functions, we can calculate its
H^{1}(\Lambda)
norm, and we also use
H^{1}(\Lambda)
to represent the set of functions with finite
H^{1}(\Lambda)
norm. Despite of the natural definition of
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda)
, the function space
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})
is the proper analogue of the function space
H^{1}_{0}
in the domain
\Lambda
. To see this, we can verify the following identity easily

(1.42)

\displaystyle\forall\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}),\qquad\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{1}(\Lambda)}=\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{1}(\Lambda^{\prime})}.

This is the important extension property of
H^{1}_{0}
function, but a general
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda)
function does not necessarily satisfy it. We will not use the notation
H^{1}_{0}(\Lambda)
in the paragraphs for the conciseness of notation, while we keep in mind that
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})
plays the same role.

Viewing the discussion above, we define the space of harmonic functions with respect to the Kawasaki dynamics

(1.43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\Lambda):=\{u\in H^{1}(\Lambda):\forall v\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-}),\left\langle{v(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=0\}.

Note that
u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda)
does
not
imply that
u\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda)
and it can have dependence on the configuration outside
\Lambda
.

1.4.4.
Operators

The translation operator, exchange operator and Kawasaki operator are respectively defined in (
1.6
), (
1.7
), (
1.1
) and (
1.5
). For
\eta\in\mathcal{X}
and
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we define
(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda)
as the configuration restricted on
\Lambda
that

(1.44)

\displaystyle\forall x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},\qquad(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda)_{x}:=\eta_{x}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{x\in\Lambda\right\}}.

We sometimes identify
\eta\in\mathcal{X}
as
\eta=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\eta_{x}\delta_{x}
for the convenience to manipulate.

The affine function defined by

(1.45)

\displaystyle\ell_{p}(\eta):=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}(p\cdot x)\,\eta_{x},

is just a formal sum as there are infinite terms, while
\pi_{b}\ell_{p}
is well-defined as

(1.46)

\displaystyle\forall b=\{x,y\}\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*},\qquad(\pi_{b}\ell_{p})(\eta):=p\cdot(y-x)(\eta_{x}-\eta_{y}).

A rigorous version of (
1.45
) is a sum restricted on the finite set
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}

(1.47)

\displaystyle\ell_{p,\Lambda}(\eta):=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}(p\cdot x)\,\eta_{x}.

In Kawasaki dynamics, we define the tangent field along the direction
e_{i}
at
x
for
u:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
as

(1.48)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x,e_{i}}u:=(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{e_{i}}).

Some simple calculation gives us

(1.49)

\displaystyle(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{e_{i}})(\eta)=\left(u(\eta^{x,x+e_{i}})-u(\eta)\right)(\eta_{x}-\eta_{x+e_{i}}),

so the term is non-zero if and only if
(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(1,0)
or
(\eta_{x},\eta_{x+e_{i}})=(0,1)
. Moreover, for both two non-zero cases, they evaluate the change that a particle jumps from
x
to
x+e_{i}
. Similarly, we define the gradient field of
u
at
x
as

(1.50)

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}u:=(\nabla_{x,e_{1}}u,\nabla_{x,e_{2}}u,\cdots,\nabla_{x,e_{d}}u).

For every
p\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, we also obtain that

(1.51)

\displaystyle p\cdot\nabla_{x}u=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}u)(\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}\ell_{p}).

The Glauber operator appears naturally in some steps of analysis. We denote by
\eta^{x}
the flip operator at
x
that

\displaystyle(\eta^{x})_{z}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\eta_{z},&\qquad z\neq x;\\
1-\eta_{z},&\qquad z=x.\end{array}\right.

Then the Glauber derivative for
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

(1.54)

\displaystyle\pi_{x}f:=f(\eta^{x})-f(\eta).

Clearly,
(\pi_{x}f)^{2}
is independent of
\eta_{x}
.

1.4.5.
Constants

We usually use
C
to represent a positive finite constant and
C(\cdots)
to indicate its dependence on other parameters. The value of
C
may change from line to line. The following constants will be fixed and used throughout the paper.

•

d\in\mathbb{N}_{+}=\{1,2,3,\cdots\}
for the dimension of lattice.

•

\lambda
for the upper bound of the jump rate, i.e.
c_{b}(\eta)\leqslant\lambda
for any
b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*},\eta\in\mathcal{X}
.

•

\mathbf{r}
for the radius of dependence of jump rate as indicated in Hypothesis
1.1
.

2.
Analytic tools

In this part, we collect all the necessary analytic tools in this paper. The two main results of this section are the modified Caccioppoli inequality (Proposition
2.6
), and the weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality (Proposition
2.12
and then Proposition
3.9
).

2.1.
Analytic tools on Kawasaki dynamics

2.1.1.
Glauber operator meets Kawasaki operator

Our first inequality comes from the observation in
[
48
, eq.(B.2)]
, which states that we can exchange the site of the Glauber derivative by paying the error of the Kawasaki operator.

Lemma 2.1
.

Recall the
L^{2}
function space defined in Section
1.4.3
, then we have

(2.1)

\displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|{\pi_{y}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\left\|{\pi_{x,y}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

As we know,
(\pi_{x}f)^{2}
does not depend on
\eta_{x}
, thus we decompose
\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}
with respect to the state of
\eta_{y}

\displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}=\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}f\right)^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{y}=1\right\}}+\left(\pi_{x}f\right)^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\eta_{y}=0\right\}}}\right\rangle_{\rho}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We denote by
\widetilde{\eta}=(\eta_{z})_{z\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\setminus\{x,y\}}
and
F(\eta_{x},\eta_{y},\widetilde{\eta})=f(\eta)
. Then we have

\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\\
=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}p(F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-p)(F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We apply the triangle inequality for this norm. The trick is that we only replace the term involving
\eta_{x}\neq\eta_{y}
. For example, in the terms
(F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}
, we add an intermediate term
F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta})
. This follows exactly the spirit of the Kawasaki operator
\pi_{x,y}
and we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}p(F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-p)(F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}p(F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-p)(F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}p(F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-p)(F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}(F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We notice the identity in the last equation

\displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{y}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}p(F(1,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}+(1-p)(F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,0,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x,y}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathcal{X}}2p(1-p)(F(1,0,\widetilde{\eta})-F(0,1,\widetilde{\eta}))^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\widetilde{\eta})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

then we conclude the desired result.
∎

2.1.2.
Spectral inequality

The spectral inequality is an important tool to analyze Markov processes. In this part, we summarize several spectral inequalities from the literature.

The most used spectral inequality is the one for independent random variables known as Efron–Stein inequality. Here we state it and its reverse version.

Lemma 2.2

(Efron–Stein inequality)
.

Let
(X_{i})_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
be i.i.d. random variables taking value on
E
, and we denote by

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(i)}[\cdot]:=\int_{E}(\cdot)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{X_{i}},\qquad\mathbb{E}_{(-i)}[\cdot]:=\int_{E^{n-1}}(\cdot)\,\prod_{1\leqslant j\leqslant n,j\neq i}{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{X_{j}},

and
\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{(i)},\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{(-i)}
for the corresponding variances. Then for a random variable
f(X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n})
, we have

(2.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{(i)}[\mathbb{E}_{(-i)}[f]]\leqslant\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}[f]\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{(-i)}[\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{(i)}[f]].

Proof.

The upper bound is the classical Efron–Stein inequality, and one can find its proof in
[
18
, Theorem 3.1]
. The lower bound, which could be seen as a reverse Efron–Stein inequality, is less well-known, but follows exactly the same strategy of proof. The authors learn the lower bound at first in
[
73
]
.
∎

A direct corollary of Efron–Stein inequality is the spectral inequality of the Glauber operator (
1.4.4
) under product Bernoulli measure.

Corollary 2.3

(Spectral inequality for Glauber dynamics)
.

For any
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we have

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho,\Lambda}[f]\leqslant\chi(\rho)\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho,\Lambda}.

Proof.

We apply the classical Efron–Stein inequality and the upper bound is the right-hand side of (
2.4
).
∎

With some more treatment, we can also obtain the spectral inequality for the Kawasaki operator (
1.5
) under product Bernoulli measure.

Lemma 2.4
.

For any bounded set
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and
f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})
, we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho}[f]\leqslant\diam(\Lambda)^{2}\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{b}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

Proof.

We apply at first the spectral inequality for Glauber dynamics

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho}[f]\leqslant\chi(\rho)\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

We fix a direction in the canonical basis
e_{i}
, then for every
x\in\Lambda
, there exists a positive integer
\ell
depending on
x
,

\displaystyle\ell(x):=\min\{k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}:x+ke_{i}\in\partial\Lambda\}.

Then we apply Lemma
2.1

\displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|{\pi_{x+e_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\left\|{\pi_{x,x+e_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

We sum this inequality along the path
x\to x+e_{i}\to x+2e_{i}\cdots\to x+\ell(x)e_{i}

\displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|{\pi_{x+\ell(x)e_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)}\left\|{\pi_{x+(j-1)e_{i},x+je_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Notice that
x+\ell(x)e_{i}\in\partial\Lambda
and
f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})
, so
f
does not depend on
\eta_{x+\ell(x)e_{i}}
and
\pi_{x+\ell(x)e_{i}}f=0
. This implies

(2.7)

\displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)}\left\|{\pi_{x+(j-1)e_{i},x+je_{i}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

We put (
2.7
) back to (
2.6
) and apply Cauchy–Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho}[f]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\ell(x)\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)}\left\langle{(\pi_{x+(j-1)e_{i},x+je_{i}}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\diam(\Lambda)\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)}\left\langle{(\pi_{x+(j-1)e_{i},x+je_{i}}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

Here the factor
\chi(\rho)
in (
2.7
) and (
2.6
) compensates, and we also make use of the fact
\ell(x)\leqslant\diam(\Lambda)
. We now exchange the order of the sum
\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(x)}

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho}[f]\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\diam(\Lambda)\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}\sum_{x\in\Lambda:\exists j\in\mathbb{N}_{+},x+je_{i}\in b}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

Because every bond
b
can be counted at most
\diam(\Lambda)
times along the direction
e_{i}
, we obtain the desired result.
∎

In
[
58
, Theorem 1]
, Lu and Yau proved a generalized version of the spectral inequality for the Glauber dynamics. We do not need that one in this paper, but we will make use of
[
58
, Theorem 2]
, the spectral inequality for Kawasaki dynamics under canonical ensemble.

Lemma 2.5

(Theorem 2,
[
58
]
)
.

There exists a positive constant
C=C(d)
, such that for any
L\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+},N\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}|
, we have

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\Lambda_{L},N}[f]\leqslant CL^{2}\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{L})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},N}.

2.1.3.
Modified Caccioppoli inequality

The modified Caccioppoli inequality is a key input to gain the convergence rate in the interacting particle systems. It is at first proved in
[
35
, Proposition 3.9]
and here we present its version in Kawasaki dynamics. The conditional expectation operator will be used in the following paragraphs. Recall
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}
defined in Section
1.4.2
. For
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and
f\in L^{1}
, we define

(2.9)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda}f:=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[f\,|\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda}\right].

Concretely, using the restriction operator defined in (
1.44
), it is calculated as

\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda}f(\eta)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda+\eta^{\prime}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda^{c})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\eta^{\prime}).

We usually denote by
{\mathsf{A}}_{L}f\equiv{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{L}}f
for short.

Proposition 2.6

(Modified Caccioppoli inequality)
.

There exists
{\theta(d,\lambda)\in(0,1)}
, finite positive constants
C(d,\lambda)
, and
R_{0}(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})
such that for every
L\geqslant R_{0}
and
{u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{3L})}
(defined in (
1.43
)), we have

(2.10)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\left\langle{{\mathsf{A}}_{L+2\mathbf{r}}u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L}}{\mathsf{A}}_{L+2\mathbf{r}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\frac{CL^{-2}}{|\Lambda_{3L}|}\left\langle{u^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\frac{\theta}{|\Lambda_{3L}|}\left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{3L}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

Its proof is similar to
[
35
, Proposition 3.9]
, which can be summarized as following three steps.

(1)

Test the harmonic function
{u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{3L})}
with its cutoff version
{\mathsf{A}}_{L}u
.

(2)

Obtain the
L^{2}
-term using the quadratic variation of the martingale
({\mathsf{A}}_{n}u)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
.

(3)

Bootstrap the result from its weak version with a correct normalization factor of volume.

The first and second step can be seen as the main difference between particle system and PDE setting, where we make the cutoff in order to reduce the influence of particles from the boundary, and we also need the nice
L^{2}
-isometry of martingale to recover the
L^{2}
term of
u
.

In Kawasaki dynamics, the quadratic variation structure is less obvious compared to the setting of continuous configuration space in
[
35
]
. Let us make some explicit calculation at first. It is clear that

(2.11)

\begin{split}\forall b\in(\Lambda_{n})^{*},\qquad&\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f={\mathsf{A}}_{n}\pi_{b}f,\\
\forall b\in(\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*},\qquad&\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f=0.\end{split}

That is to say, the operators
{\mathsf{A}}_{n}
and
\pi_{b}
are commutative when the bond
b
stays in
(\Lambda_{n})^{*}
, and the influence is
0
when
b
is outside
\Lambda_{n}
. When
b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}
, the situation is subtle as we will see the perturbation

\displaystyle\forall x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x,\qquad\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta)={\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta^{x,y})-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta).

Since we apply the conditional expectation, the information of
\eta_{x}
is no longer accessible in
{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta^{x,y})
and we have

\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta^{x,y})={\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}(\Lambda_{n}\setminus\{x\})+\eta_{y}\delta_{x}).

Thus, near the boundary the Kawasaki operator is like resampling the state at
x
and we have

(2.12)

\displaystyle\forall x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x,\qquad\left\langle{(\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}=2\chi(\rho)\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

By the spectral inequality (
2.4
), we know that

\displaystyle\chi(\rho)\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n}}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\geqslant\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n}f-{\mathsf{A}}_{n-2}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

The inequality is not on the desired direction, because we hope to give an upper bound for the boundary perturbation. For this reason, we would like to study how to control this Glauber derivative near the boundary at first.

Lemma 2.7
.

For
{\mathsf{A}}_{n}\equiv{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}}
defined in (
2.9
) and
f\in L^{2}
, the following estimate holds

(2.13)

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant 4\left(\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right).

Proof.

The left-hand side can be expressed with (
2.12
)

(2.14)

\begin{split}\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}&=\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}\left\langle{(\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
&=\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}2\chi(\rho)\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split}

We notice that

(2.15)

\begin{split}\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta)&={\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta^{x})-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f(\eta)\\
&=\int_{\{0,1\}}\left({\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f(\eta^{x})-{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f(\eta)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\eta_{y})\\
&=\int_{\{0,1\}}\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(\eta_{y}).\end{split}

Using Jensen’s inequality, we have

(2.16)

\displaystyle\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

Then we apply Lemma
2.1
to
{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f
and obtain that

(2.17)

\displaystyle\left\|{\pi_{x}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|{\pi_{y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\chi(\rho)}}\left\|{\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f}\right\|_{L^{2}}.

We put (
2.17
) and (
2.16
) back to (
2.14
), and obtain that

(2.18)

\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
\leqslant\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}4\chi(\rho)\left(\left\langle{\left(\pi_{y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right).

For the first term on right-hand side, it is exactly the fluctuation on
\partial\Lambda_{n+2}
, so we apply the reverse Efron–Stein inequality (the first inequality of (
2.2
)) to
{\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f
under the expectation over the
\{\eta_{y}\}_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}\chi(\rho)\left\langle{\left(\pi_{y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}[\mathbb{E}_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}[{\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f\,|\eta_{y}]]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}[{\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f]

\displaystyle=\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}.

Recall that
\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}
is defined in Section
1.4.2
, and note that
\chi(\rho)
also appears similarly in (
2.4
) in a reversed inequality. We also use the identity
\mathbb{E}_{\rho,\partial\Lambda_{n+2}}[{\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f\,|\eta_{y}]={\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f
here. Then we take the expectation of other variables to yield the estimate of the first term on the right-hand sideof (
2.18
)

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}4\chi(\rho)\left\langle{\left(\pi_{y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant 4\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}f-{\mathsf{A}}_{n}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

For the second term on the right-hand sideof (
2.18
), we apply (
2.11
) and once again Jensen’s inequality to obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}\left\langle{\left(\pi_{x,y}{\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_{n},y\notin\Lambda_{n},y\sim x}\left\langle{\left({\mathsf{A}}_{\Lambda_{n}\sqcup\{y\}}\pi_{x,y}f\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{n+2}\pi_{b}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

This concludes the desired result.
∎

Once we develop Lemma
2.7
, the rest of the proof follows that in
[
35
, Proposition 3.9]
.

Proof of Proposition
2.6
.

The proof can be divided into three steps.

Step 1: construction of test function.
In the first step, we do some preparation. Our object is to regularize
u
such that it becomes a function in
\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda_{3L}^{-})
. A very natural idea is to apply the conditional expectation operator (
2.9
), then the information outside
3L
will be averaged. In order to make this cutoff more smooth, we propose the following regularized version

(2.19)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}f:=\frac{1}{\ell}\int_{0}^{\ell}{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}f\,{\mathrm{d}}t.

Recall
\Lambda_{L}
is defined for
L\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, so
{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}
does for
s,t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Its Kawasaki derivative can be calculated using (
2.11
)

\displaystyle\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}f=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\pi_{b}f&\text{if }b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*};\\
\frac{1}{\ell}\int_{\tau(b)+}^{s+\ell}{\mathsf{A}}_{t}\pi_{b}f\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\frac{(\tau_{b}-s)\wedge 1}{\ell}\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{\tau_{b}}f\mathbf{1}_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{\tau(b)},\Lambda_{\tau(b)}^{c}\right)^{*}}&\text{if }b\in\left(\Lambda_{s},\Lambda_{s+\ell}^{c}\right)^{*};\\
0&\text{if }b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell}^{c})^{*}.\end{array}\right.

Here the notation
\tau(b)
is defined as

(2.23)

\displaystyle\tau(b):=\inf\{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\}.

From the definition of hypercube in Section
1.4.1
, we know
b\in\Lambda_{\tau(b)+}
but
b\notin\Lambda_{\tau(b)}
.

We will also make use of the following operator

(2.24)

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}f:=({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\circ{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell})(f)=\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t){\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}f\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

The motivation comes from the following identity that

(2.25)

\displaystyle\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{f(\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}f)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t)\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}f)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t.

Similar to (
2.1.3
), we also calculate its Kawasaki derivative as preparation

(2.26)

\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}f=\\
\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\pi_{b}f&\text{if }b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*};\\
\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{\tau(b)+}^{s+\ell}(s+\ell-t){\mathsf{A}}_{t}\pi_{b}f\,{\mathrm{d}}t&\\
\qquad\qquad+\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{(\tau(b)-2)\vee s}^{\tau(b)}(s+\ell-t)\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{\tau(b)}f\mathbf{1}_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{\tau(b)},\Lambda_{\tau(b)}^{c}\right)^{*}}&\text{if }b\in\left(\Lambda_{s},\Lambda_{s+\ell}^{c}\right)^{*};\\
0&\text{if }b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell}^{c})^{*}.\end{array}\right.

Step 2: week Caccioppoli inequality.
We then prove the weak Caccioppoli inequality at first. Fix
\theta^{\prime}(\lambda):=\frac{10\lambda}{1+10\lambda}\in(0,1)
; recall that
\lambda\geqslant 1
is the constant in Hypothesis
1.1
. For every
L>0
,
s\geqslant L+2\mathbf{r},\ell>1,s+\ell<3L
and
{u\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda_{s+\ell+2})}
, we claim that

(2.27)

\ell^{-2}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\left\langle{{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L}}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
\leqslant\theta^{\prime}\left(\ell^{-2}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{s+\ell}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right).

The main idea is to use the conditional expectation
\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u
given in (
2.24
), because it provides a cutoff that
\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda_{s+\ell+2}^{-})
. Then we test it with
u

\displaystyle 0=\left\langle{\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u(-\mathcal{L}_{s+\ell+2}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell+2})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{II}+\mathbf{III}.

Here decompose the right-hand side into the sum of three terms

(2.28)

\begin{split}\mathbf{I}&:=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho},\\
\mathbf{II}&:=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho},\\
\mathbf{III}&:=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho},\end{split}

and we have the estimate

(2.29)

\displaystyle|\mathbf{I}|\leqslant|\mathbf{II}|+|\mathbf{III}|.

The term
\mathbf{I}
is the easiest one to treat

(2.30)

\begin{split}\mathbf{I}&=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
&=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u){\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}(c_{b}\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
&=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split}

From the first line to the second line, we use the fact
\widetilde{{\mathsf{A}}}_{s,\ell}={\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\circ{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}
and the reversibility of
{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}
. From the second line to the third line, we use the fact
c_{b}
is
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{s}}
-measurable when
b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}
and
{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\pi_{b}=\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}
from (
2.26
).

The identity (
2.30
) does not apply directly to
\mathbf{II}
, because for
b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}
, the jump rate
c_{b}
is no longer
\mathscr{F}_{\Lambda_{s}}
-measurable. Therefore, we make use of the exact expression (
2.24
)

(2.31)

\begin{split}|\mathbf{II}|&=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left|\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t)\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right|\\
&\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\frac{\lambda}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t)\left\langle{(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}u)^{2}+(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\frac{2\lambda}{\ell^{2}}\int_{0}^{\ell}(\ell-t)\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\lambda\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split}

From the first line to the second line, we make use of Young’s inequality and
c_{b}\leqslant\lambda
. From the second line to the third line,
{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}\pi_{b}=\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}
and
\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+t}\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}
is also applied thanks to Jensen’s inequality.

The term
\mathbf{III}
has two integrals following (
2.26
), which can be noted respectively by
\mathbf{III.1}
and
\mathbf{III.2}
. The first part is similar to (
2.31
)

(2.32)

\begin{split}|\mathbf{III.1}|&\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\left|\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{\tau(b)+}^{s+\ell}(s+\ell-t)\left\langle{c_{b}({\mathsf{A}}_{t}\pi_{b}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right|\\
&\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\lambda\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split}

The second part is the key to make appear the
L^{2}
term

\begin{split}|\mathbf{III.2}|&\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\left|\frac{2}{\ell^{2}}\int_{(\tau(b)-2)\vee s}^{\tau(b)}(s+\ell-t)\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{\tau(b)}u)(\pi_{b}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\mathbf{1}_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{\tau(b)},\Lambda_{\tau(b)}^{c}\right)^{*}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right|\\
&\leqslant\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s})^{*}}\frac{2\lambda}{\ell}\left\langle{\gamma^{-1}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{\tau(b)}u)^{2}+\gamma(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\mathbf{1}_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{\tau(b)},\Lambda_{\tau(b)}^{c}\right)^{*}}.\end{split}

From the first line to the second line, Young’s inequality is applied with
\gamma>0
to be fixed, together with the trivial bound
(s+\ell-t)\leqslant\ell
for
t
defied above. We rearrange the sum, and obtain

(2.33)

\begin{split}|\mathbf{III.2}|&\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\ell\rfloor-2}\sum_{b\in\left(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c}\right)^{*}}\frac{2\lambda}{\ell}\left\langle{\gamma^{-1}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{n}u)^{2}+\gamma(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
&\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\ell\rfloor-2}\left(\frac{8\lambda}{\gamma\ell}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+n+2}u-{\mathsf{A}}_{s+n}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left(\frac{8\lambda}{\gamma\ell}+\frac{2\gamma\lambda}{\ell}\right)\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right)\\
&=\frac{8\lambda}{\gamma\ell}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\ell}u-{\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\ell\rfloor-2}\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{n},\Lambda_{n}^{c})^{*}}\left(\frac{8\lambda}{\gamma\ell}+\frac{2\gamma\lambda}{\ell}\right)\left\langle{(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split}

Here we insert the estimate from Lemma
2.7
in the second line to handle the perturbation of boundary term, and then make use of the orthogonal decomposition of martingale from the second line to the third line. We choose
\gamma=\ell
, and put (
2.30
), (
2.31
), (
2.32
) and (
2.33
) back to (
2.29
), which concludes that

(2.34)

\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
\leqslant 10\lambda\left(\ell^{-2}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\ell}u-{\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}\setminus(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right).

Then Widman’s filling-hole argument applies by adding
10\lambda\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}
on the two sides (Jensen’s inequality is also applied to the right-hand side)

(2.35)

(1+10\lambda)\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s-2\mathbf{r}})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}{\mathsf{A}}_{s,\ell}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
\leqslant 10\lambda\left(\ell^{-2}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{s+\ell}u-{\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{s+\ell})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right).

We note the martingale property of
({\mathsf{A}}_{s}u)_{s\geqslant 0}
and divide
(1+10\lambda)
on the two sides to obtain (
2.27
) with
\theta^{\prime}=\frac{10\lambda}{1+10\lambda}
.

Step 3: bootstrap.
When we take
L
large enough,
\ell=L
, and
s=2L
in (
2.27
), we obtain

(2.36)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\left\langle{{\mathsf{A}}_{2L}u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L}}{\mathsf{A}}_{2L}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant 3^{d}\theta^{\prime}\left(\frac{L^{-2}}{|\Lambda_{3L}|}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{3L}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{3L}|}\left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{3L}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right).

The factor before the term
\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{3L}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}
is correct, but an additional volume factor is added before
\left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{3L}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}
compared to the desired result (
2.10
). This is not good as we do not necessarily have
3^{d}\theta^{\prime}<1
. However, if we choose carefully
s=(1+\delta)L
and
\ell=\delta L
, we obtain

(2.37)

\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\left\langle{{\mathsf{A}}_{L}u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L}}{\mathsf{A}}_{L}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
\leqslant(1+2\delta)^{d}\theta^{\prime}\left(\frac{(\delta L)^{-2}}{|\Lambda_{(1+2\delta)L}|}\left\langle{({\mathsf{A}}_{(1+2\delta)L}u)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{(1+2\delta)L}|}\left\langle{u(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{(1+2\delta)L}}u)}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right).

We can choose
\delta
small such that
(1+2\delta)^{d}\theta^{\prime}<1
, and then iterate the Dirichlet energy term on the right-hand side, such that the domain increases progressively to
\Lambda_{3L}
. See
[
35
, eq.(56)-(60)]
for details, since this step is an algebraic iteration and independent of model.
∎

2.2.
Analytic tools on independent particles

In this part, we develop the analytic tools on independent particles. We denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}:=\mathbb{N}^{{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}
for the configuration space of independent particles on
{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, which does not have constraint for the number of particles on every site. For
\widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
such that
\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\geqslant 1
, we define the jump operator

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}^{x,y}:=\widetilde{\eta}-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y},

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\pi}_{x,y}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta}):=\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta}^{x,y})-\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta}).

The generator of the independent particles is

(2.38)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\widetilde{u})(\widetilde{\eta}):=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\widetilde{\eta}_{x}\sum_{y\sim x}\widetilde{\pi}_{x,y}\widetilde{u}.

This generates a dynamic that every particle jumps independently with rate
1
to the nearest neighbor site.

Fix an
\alpha>0
, we denote by
\text{Poi}(\alpha)
the Poisson distribution on
\mathbb{N}
with mean
\alpha
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha}:=\text{Poi}(\alpha)^{\otimes{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}
the probability measure on
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
which is stationary with respect to
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
in (
2.38
), and
\left\llangle{\cdot}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}
its associated expectation. We usually write
\widetilde{\eta}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
as a canonical random variable sampled under
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha}
. We also denote by
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\Lambda,N}
and
\left\llangle{\cdot}\right\rrangle_{\Lambda,N}
for the probability and expectation under the canonical ensemble, i.e.
N
particles distributed independently and uniformly in
\Lambda
.

2.2.1.
Mecke’s identity

The first lemma is about Mecke’s identity, which simplifies some expectation under
\left\llangle{\cdot}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}
by adding one additional particle. This identity is inspired from the reference
[
55
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Lemma 2.8

(Mecke’s identity)
.

For any
\alpha>0
and
F:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
integrable under
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
, then for every
x\in\Lambda
, the following identity holds

(2.39)

\displaystyle\left\llangle{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}F(\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}=\alpha\left\llangle{F(\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x},x)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}.

Proof.

We make the calculation directly

\displaystyle\left\llangle{\widetilde{\eta}_{x}F(\widetilde{\eta},x)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=e^{-\alpha}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{k}}{k!}\left\llangle{kF(\widetilde{\eta},x)\,|\widetilde{\eta}_{x}=k}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\alpha e^{-\alpha}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{(k-1)}}{(k-1)!}\left\llangle{F(\widetilde{\eta},x)\,|\widetilde{\eta}_{x}=k}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\alpha e^{-\alpha}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{(k-1)}}{(k-1)!}\left\llangle{F(\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x},x)\,|\widetilde{\eta}_{x}=k-1}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\alpha e^{-\alpha}\left\llangle{F(\widetilde{\eta}+\delta_{x},x)}\right\rrangle_{\alpha}.

∎

The calculation over independent particle system has a close connection with the finite difference operator on lattice
{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
or
\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L}
. Given a function
\widetilde{u}:\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
which only depends on the configuration in
\Lambda
, and using the expression
\widetilde{\eta}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.02773pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=4.30554pt}\Lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}
, then we have the following canonical projection

(2.40)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{N}(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{N}):=\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta}).

Moreover,
\widetilde{u}
is a function on
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
if and only if
\widetilde{u}_{N}
is invariant under permutation for all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
; see
[
35
, Lemma A.1]
for similar discussions.

We state some more properties using the expression (
2.40
). To better treat the high dimensional function, we define the following notation for shorthand,

\displaystyle\strokedint_{\Lambda^{N}}\left(\cdot\right):=\frac{1}{|\Lambda|^{N}}\sum_{x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}\in\Lambda}\left(\cdot\right),

then using the notation (
2.40
), we observe

(2.41)

\displaystyle\left\llangle{\widetilde{u}}\right\rrangle_{\Lambda,N}=\strokedint_{\Lambda^{N}}\widetilde{u}_{N}.

For any integer
1\leqslant i\leqslant N
and
e\in U:=\{\pm e_{1},\pm e_{2},\cdots,\pm e_{d}\}
, the finite difference operator
\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}
is defined for
\widetilde{u}_{N}
as

(2.42)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\widetilde{u}_{N})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}):=\widetilde{u}_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i}+e,\cdots,x_{N})-\widetilde{u}_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i},\cdots,x_{N}),

which is commutative in the sense

(2.43)

\displaystyle\forall 1\leqslant i,j\leqslant N,\forall e,e^{\prime}\in U,\qquad\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}=\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}.

Combing the canonical projection (
2.40
), the finite difference operator is related to the generator
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
in (
2.38
) by the following identity

(2.44)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\widetilde{u}(\widetilde{\eta})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}\widetilde{u}_{N}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N}).

Then a lot of analytic tools on Euclidean space can be applied to independent particles.

2.2.2.
H^{2}
-estimate

In the following paragraphs, we will recall
H^{2}
estimate for independent particles (indeed identity), which will be used in the multiscale Poincaré estimate later in Proposition
2.12
. The proof follows
[
11
, Lemma B.19]
and
[
17
, Proposition 3.10]
after a careful review. We will also explain in detail in Remark
2.10
the difficulty met when developing the counterpart for Kawasaki dynamics.

Lemma 2.9

(Dimension-free
H^{2}
estimate on torus)
.

For any
L,N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and any function
u,f:(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

(2.45)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u=f,

the following identity holds

(2.46)

\displaystyle\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\sum_{e,e^{\prime}\in U}(\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u)^{2}=4\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}f^{2}.

Proof.

It is easy to verify the identity

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e},

so (
2.45
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u=f.

We evaluate the
L^{2}
sum of the two sides

(2.47)

\begin{split}\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}f^{2}&=\frac{1}{4}\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\right)^{2}\\
&=\frac{1}{4}\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{e\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in U}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},-e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}u\right).\end{split}

On torus, it is easy to verify the integration by part formula for
u,v:(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\forall 1\leqslant i\leqslant N,e\in U,\qquad\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u)v=\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}u(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}v).

We apply it and the commutativity (
2.43
) to the right-hand side of (
2.47
)

\displaystyle\strokedint_{(\mathbb{T}^{d}_{L})^{N}}\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},-e}\mathcal{D}_{x_{i},e}u\right)\left(\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},-e^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{x_{j},e^{\prime}}u\right)
