Under this assumption, the contribution of the diagonal terms are

(6.62)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n},\\
\dist(z,\partial{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})>3^{n}\end{subarray}}\left\langle{\left(\Delta_{M,z}\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}\setminus\{y_{1}\},M}

\displaystyle\leqslant 20\lambda\cdot 3^{d(m-n)}\cdot 3^{(2d+2)n}\cdot 3^{-dm}\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}

\displaystyle=20\lambda\cdot 3^{(d+2)n}\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}.

Step 3.2: the off-diagonal term
\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}\setminus\{y_{1}\},M}
.
Before starting the proof, we remark that the error in each off-diagonal term should be smaller than that of the diagonal term. A naive application of Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and (
6.60
) does not work here, because the factor
3^{-dm}
in (
6.60
) is not enough to dominate the total number of
3^{2d(m-n)}
off-diagonal terms.

We observe that
\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}\setminus\{y_{1}\},M}
is actually asymptotically independent, and we need a decoupling inequality to justify it. Thus we make the following decomposition

(6.63)

\begin{split}&\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}\setminus\{y_{1}\},M}\\
&=\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{\frac{M}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}|-1}}+\left(\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}\setminus\{y_{1}\},M}-\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{\frac{M}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}|-1}}\right)\\
&=\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}}\right\rangle^{2}_{\frac{M}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}|-1}}+\left(\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}\setminus\{y_{1}\},M}-\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{\frac{M}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}|-1}}\right).\end{split}

From the second line to the third line, we use the fact that under
\mathbb{P}_{\rho}
, the variables
\Delta_{M,z}
and
\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}
are independent and of the same law. We continue to estimate the two terms respectively.

For the first term in (
6.63
), we make use of the definition (
6.58
). It transforms the estimate as the regularity of mean

\displaystyle\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}}\right\rangle^{2}_{\frac{M}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}|-1}}=\left\langle{\phi_{\hat{\rho}^{\prime},z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},\xi}-\phi_{\hat{\rho},z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},\xi}}\right\rangle^{2}_{\hat{\rho}^{\prime\prime}},

so (
6.10
) applies

(6.64)

\begin{split}\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}}\right\rangle^{2}_{\frac{M}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}|-1}}&\leqslant 2\left\langle{\phi_{\hat{\rho}^{\prime},z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},\xi}}\right\rangle^{2}_{\hat{\rho}^{\prime\prime}}+2\left\langle{\phi_{\hat{\rho},z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},\xi}}\right\rangle^{2}_{\hat{\rho}^{\prime\prime}}\\
&\leqslant 2\lambda\cdot 3^{(d+2)n}\left((\widetilde{\Theta}_{\hat{\rho},\hat{\rho}^{\prime\prime}}^{(3^{n}+2\mathbf{r})^{d}}-1)^{2}+(\widetilde{\Theta}_{\hat{\rho}^{\prime},\hat{\rho}^{\prime\prime}}^{(3^{n}+2\mathbf{r})^{d}}-1)^{2}\right)\\
&\leqslant 4\lambda\cdot 3^{(d+2)n}\cdot\left(\left(1+3^{-dm}\varepsilon^{-1}\right)^{3^{dn}}-1\right)^{2}\\
&\leqslant 4\lambda\cdot 3^{(3d+2)n}\cdot 3^{-2dm}\cdot\varepsilon^{-2}.\end{split}

Here from the second line to the third line, we use the estimate of the two-sided bias factor in (
6.57
) for
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}^{-})
. We also need to assume (
6.61
) from the third line to the forth line. Compare the regularity of mean (
6.10
) and of
L^{2}
(
6.11
), we gain another factor of type
(\widetilde{\Theta}_{\hat{\rho},\hat{\rho}^{\prime\prime}}^{(3^{n}+2\mathbf{r})^{d}}-1)
, that is why we have
3^{-2dm}
in the last line.

For the second term in (
6.63
), we use the local equivalence of ensembles. Especially, since
\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}
is not necessarily positive, we need apply the version of (
6.19
), which requires some supplementary conditions. These conditions are satisfied as we recall that
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}^{-})
and assume (
6.61
). Then we obtain

(6.65)

\begin{split}&\left|\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}\setminus\{y_{1}\},M}-\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{\frac{M}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}|-1}}\right|\\
&\leqslant 3^{d(2n-m)}\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\left\langle{|\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}|}\right\rangle_{\frac{M}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}|-1}}\\
&\leqslant 3^{d(2n-m)}\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\cdot\left\langle{(\Delta_{M,z})^{2}}\right\rangle_{\frac{M}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}|-1}}\\
&\leqslant 20\lambda\cdot 3^{(4d+2)n}\cdot 3^{-2dm}\cdot\varepsilon^{-2}.\end{split}

Here we use Cauchy–Schwarz inequality from the second line to the third line, and insert the estimate (
6.60
) in the last line. Finally, we also gain the factor
3^{-2dm}
.

We combine the estimates (
6.64
) and (
6.65
) and obtain an upper bound for the off-diagonal terms

(6.66)

\begin{split}&\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z,z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}_{m,n},z\neq z^{\prime}\\
\dist(z,\partial{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})>3^{n},\dist(z^{\prime},\partial{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})>3^{n}\end{subarray}}\left\langle{\Delta_{M,z}\Delta_{M,z^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}^{-}_{m}\setminus\{y_{1}\},M}\right|\\
&\leqslant 20\lambda\cdot 3^{2d(m-n)}\cdot 3^{(4d+2)n}\cdot 3^{-2dm}\cdot\varepsilon^{-2}\\
&=20\lambda\cdot 3^{(2d+2)n}\cdot\varepsilon^{-2}.\end{split}

The error (
6.66
) from the off-diagonal terms is the leading order compared to (
6.62
) from the diagonal terms. We put them back to (
6.54
) and (
6.52
), and obtain the estimates for the contribution from
\mathbf{III}

(6.67)

\displaystyle\mathbf{III}\leqslant 20\lambda\cdot 3^{-m}\cdot\left(3^{(2d+2)n}\cdot\varepsilon^{-2}+3^{(d+2)n}\cdot\varepsilon^{-1}\right).

Step 4: track of parameters.
We collect all the estimates from (
6.50
), (
6.51
) and (
6.67
), and put them back to (
6.39
) to obtain

\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}^{*}}}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(\ell_{\xi}+\hat{\phi}^{(\varepsilon)}_{m,n,\xi}))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}\left(\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}\right)\xi\right)\\
\qquad+C(\lambda)\left(3^{dn}\varepsilon+3^{-dn}+3^{d(6n-m)}+3^{(n-m)}+3^{(2d+2)n-m}\varepsilon^{-2}\right)\mathsf{Id}.

The choice of parameters should satisfy the assumptions (
6.41
), (
6.43
), (
6.61
), and also make the upper bound above be small. A possible choice is

\displaystyle\varepsilon=3^{-2dn},\qquad n=\left\lfloor\frac{m}{9d+3}\right\rfloor,

which gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}^{*}}}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(\ell_{\xi}+\hat{\phi}^{(\varepsilon)}_{m,n,\xi}))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}\left(\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}\right)\xi\right)+C(\lambda)3^{-\frac{m}{12}}.

Then we apply the uniform estimate from Proposition
5.3
, which gives us

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}^{*}}}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(\ell_{\xi}+\hat{\phi}^{(\varepsilon)}_{m,n,\xi}))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}\left(\rho\right)\xi+C(d,\lambda)3^{-\frac{(1\wedge\gamma_{1})m}{12}}.

Together with (
6.38
), this concludes (
6.37
) by setting
\gamma_{4}:=\frac{(1\wedge\gamma_{1})}{12}
.

∎

Proof of Proposition
6.6
.

Viewing Proposition
4.1
and Corollary
4.3
, for any
{v\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(\ell_{\xi}+v))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda)\xi\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)\xi,

thus
\mu(\Lambda,\xi)\geqslant 0
. Moreover, Proposition
6.7
implies

\displaystyle 0\leqslant\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\xi)\leqslant\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}|}\left\langle{\sum_{b\in\overline{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}^{*}}}\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}(\ell_{\xi}+\hat{\phi}^{(\varepsilon)}_{m,n,\xi}))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}-\frac{1}{2}\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)\xi\leqslant C3^{-\gamma_{4}m},

then we prove (
6.33
) along a subsequence
\{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
. Like (4) of Proposition
4.1
, we can prove that
\mu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},\xi)
is a subadditive quantity, so Lemma
A.1
applies and generalizes the uniform convergence (
6.33
) to general cubes
\Lambda_{L}
with
\gamma_{3}:=\gamma_{4}
.
∎

6.4.
Stationary corrector

Recall that we have two definitions of the conductivity, that is (
1.15
) for
\mathbf{c}(\rho)
and (
5.2
) for
\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
. In this part, we will establish the identity
\mathbf{c}(\rho)=\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
, so the results proved in the previous sections are indeed for the convergence to
\mathbf{c}(\rho)
. Secondly, we will give the concrete construction of
\Phi_{L}
valid for (
1.20
).

We first establish several auxiliary lemmas on the stationary function. Recall that a mapping
y\mapsto f(y,\eta)
is stationary iff
f(y,\eta)=\tau_{y}f(0,\eta)=f(0,\tau_{y}\eta)
.

Lemma 6.8
.

The following properties hold.

(1)

For every
x,y,z\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we have

(6.68)

\displaystyle\pi_{x+z,y+z}\tau_{z}=\tau_{z}\pi_{x,y}.

(2)

For every integer
1\leqslant i\leqslant d
, every
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and every local function
f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
, the following mapping is stationary.

(6.69)

\displaystyle y\mapsto c_{y,y+e_{i}}\pi_{y,y+e_{i}}\left(\ell_{\xi}+\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{x}f\right)^{2}.

(3)

For every
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}},f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda_{L}^{-})
, we have

(6.70)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle{c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\pi_{0,e_{i}}\big(\ell_{\xi}+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{x}f\big)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\leqslant\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda_{L}^{*}}}\left\langle{c_{b}\left(\pi_{b}\left(\ell_{\xi}+f\right)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

Proof.

The identity (
6.68
) can be proved by a direct verification. To prove (
6.69
), it suffices to prove

(6.71)

\displaystyle c_{y,y+e_{i}}\pi_{y,y+e_{i}}\left(\ell_{\xi}+\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{x}f\right)^{2}=\tau_{y}\left(c_{0,0+e_{i}}\pi_{0,e_{i}}\left(\ell_{\xi}+\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{x}f\right)^{2}\right).

We start from its right-hand side and evaluate the term one by one. From the definition of
c_{y,y+e_{i}}
, we have
{\tau_{y}c_{0,0+e_{i}}=c_{y,y+e_{i}}}
. For the term
\ell_{\xi}
, we have

\displaystyle\tau_{y}\pi_{0,e_{i}}\ell_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\tau_{y}\left(-\xi\cdot e_{i}(\eta_{e_{i}}-\eta_{0})\right)=-\xi\cdot e_{i}(\eta_{y+e_{i}}-\eta_{y})=\pi_{y,y+e_{i}}\ell_{\xi}.

For the term involving
f
, we apply (
6.68
)

\displaystyle\tau_{y}\pi_{0,e_{i}}\left(\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{x}f\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{y,y+e_{i}}\tau_{y}\left(\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{x}f\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{y,y+e_{i}}\left(\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{x+y}f\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{y,y+e_{i}}\left(\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{x}f\right).

Then we obtain (
6.71
) and establish the stationary property.

Finally, we verify the inequality (
6.70
). Notice that
\pi_{y,y+z}\tau_{x}\ell_{\xi}=\pi_{y,y+z}\ell_{\xi}
, we have

(6.72)

\begin{split}&\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle{c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\pi_{0,e_{i}}\big(\ell_{\xi}+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{x}f\big)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle{c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\pi_{0,e_{i}}\big(\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{L}}\tau_{x}(\ell_{\xi}+f)\big)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\\
&\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{L}}\left\langle{c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\pi_{0,e_{i}}\left(\tau_{x}(\ell_{\xi}+f)\right)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.\end{split}

From the first line to the second line, we also use the fact that
f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda_{L}^{-})
, so the derivative vanish when translation is outside
\Lambda_{L}
. From the second line to the third line above, we apply Jensen’s inequality. Then we simplify the result

\displaystyle\left\langle{c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\pi_{0,e_{i}}\left(\tau_{x}(\ell_{\xi}+f)\right)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\tau_{-x}c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\pi_{0,e_{i}}\left(\tau_{x}(\ell_{\xi}+f)\right)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{c_{-x,-x+e_{i}}\left(\pi_{-x,-x+e_{i}}\left(\ell_{\xi}+f\right)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

The equality in the first line comes from the stationarity of
\mathbb{P}_{\rho}
, i.e.
\left\langle{\tau_{x}F}\right\rangle_{\rho}=\left\langle{F}\right\rangle_{\rho}
for all
x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, while the equality from the second line comes from (
6.68
). We put this identity back to (
6.72
) and conclude the desired result

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle{c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\pi_{0,e_{i}}\big(\ell_{\xi}+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{L}}\tau_{x}f\big)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{L}}\left\langle{c_{-x,-x+e_{i}}\left(\pi_{-x,-x+e_{i}}\left(\ell_{\xi}+f\right)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda_{L}^{*}}}\left\langle{c_{b}\left(\pi_{b}\left(\ell_{\xi}+f\right)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

∎

With the preparation of Lemma
6.8
and Proposition
6.6
, we can now prove the main result in this subsection.

Proposition 6.9
.

For every
\rho\in[0,1]
, the quantities defined in (
1.15
) and (
5.2
) coincide

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}(\rho)=\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho).

Moreover, let
\phi_{\Lambda,\xi}
be an optimizer for
\mu(\Lambda,\xi)
defined in (
6.32
) and
\Phi_{L}\in\mathcal{F}^{d}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})
be defined as

(6.73)

\displaystyle\Phi_{L}:=\left(\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\phi_{\Lambda_{L},e_{1}},\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\phi_{\Lambda_{L},e_{2}},\cdots,\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\phi_{\Lambda_{L},e_{d}}\right),

then there exists an exponent
\gamma(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})>0
and a positive constant
C(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})<\infty
, such that

(6.74)

\displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|\mathbf{c}(\rho;\Phi_{L})-\mathbf{c}(\rho)|\leqslant CL^{-\gamma}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the sandwich argument in (
6.27
) and can be divided the into three steps. Similar arguments can be found in
[
35
, Theorem B.1]
.

Step 1:
\mathbf{c}(\rho)\leqslant\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
.
We recall (
1.14
) that

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;\Phi_{L})\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle{c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\xi\cdot\left\{e_{i}(\eta_{e_{i}}-\eta_{0})-\pi_{0,e_{i}}(\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{y}\Phi_{L})\right\}\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

and notice the fact that

\displaystyle\xi\cdot x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})

\displaystyle=-\pi_{0,x}\ell_{\xi},

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\Phi_{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\phi_{\Lambda_{L},\xi},

where the second identity comes from the linear map
\xi\mapsto\phi_{\Lambda_{L},\xi}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;\Phi_{L})\xi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left\langle{c_{0,e_{i}}\left(\pi_{0,e_{i}}\left(\ell_{\xi}+\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}\tau_{y}\phi_{\Lambda_{L},\xi}\right)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{|\Lambda_{L}|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda_{L}^{*}}}\left\langle{c_{b}\left(\pi_{b}\left(\ell_{\xi}+\phi_{\Lambda_{L},\xi}\right)\right)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}

\displaystyle\leqslant\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)\xi+\mu(\Lambda_{L},\xi),

where we apply (
6.70
) from the first line to the second line and (
6.32
) from the second line to the third line. Using the estimate (
6.33
) about
\mu(\Lambda_{L},\xi)
, we obtain an important inequality chain with
C,\gamma_{3}
independent of
\rho\in[0,1]

(6.75)

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho)\xi\leqslant\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;\Phi_{L})\xi\leqslant\xi\cdot\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)\xi+CL^{-\gamma_{3}}|\xi|^{2}.

We take the first one and the third one, and let
L\nearrow\infty
, and obtain the desired result

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}(\rho)\leqslant\lim_{L\to\infty}\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda_{L})=\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho).

Step 2:
\mathbf{c}(\rho)\geqslant\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
.
We pick
F_{K}
as a sequence of local functions to approximate
\mathbf{c}(\rho)
, and compare
v_{p,K}=\ell_{p}+\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}p\cdot\tau_{x}F_{K}
in the functional of
\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda_{L},q)

\displaystyle\overline{\nu}_{*}(\rho,\Lambda_{L},q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}^{-1}(\rho,\Lambda_{L})\cdot q

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{{\Lambda}^{*}}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}v_{p,K})-\frac{1}{2}c_{b}(\pi_{b}v_{p,K})^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}.

Here we pick
F_{K}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda_{L})
. Applying the stationarity of mapping in (
6.69
) and (
3.9
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}^{-1}(\rho,\Lambda_{L})q\geqslant p\cdot q-\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)}p\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;F_{K})p.

Letting
L,K\to\infty
, this yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{D}}^{-1}(\rho)q\geqslant p\cdot q-\frac{1}{4\chi(\rho)}p\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho)p.

By taking
q={\overbracket{D}}(\rho)p
and recall
2\chi(\rho){\overbracket{D}}(\rho)=\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
from (
5.2
), we conclude
\mathbf{c}(\rho)\geqslant\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
.

Step 3: Error estimate.
Once we identify that
\mathbf{c}(\rho)=\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
, we go back to (
6.75
) to obtain

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho)\xi\leqslant\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho;\Phi_{L})\xi\leqslant\xi\cdot\mathbf{c}(\rho)\xi+CL^{-\gamma_{3}}|\xi|^{2}.

Since
C,\gamma_{3}
come from Proposition
6.6
and are independent of
\rho
, this concludes the estimate (
6.74
) by setting
\gamma:=\gamma_{3}
.
∎

6.5.
Proof of Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.4

We summarize the proof of the main theorems in this paper.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.4
.

The finite-volume approximation defined in (
1.22
) gives a subadditive quantity
\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho,\Lambda_{L})
, which defines a limit
\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
in (
5.2
). Using the dual quantity, we prove the convergence rate of this approximation in Proposition
5.1
and Proposition
6.4
. In Lemma
6.8
, we identify that
\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
coincides with
\mathbf{c}(\rho)
defined in (
1.15
), therefore Proposition
5.1
together with Proposition
6.4
proves Theorem
1.4
. Finally, Proposition
6.6
removes the dependence of density of the local corrector and Lemma
6.8
proves Theorem
1.2
, where (
6.73
) gives a concrete construction of the density-free local corrector.
∎

7.
Quantitative hydrodynamic limit

In this part, we reveal the connection between homogenization and
hydrodynamic limit. The main task for establishing the hydrodynamic limit for non-gradient models
lies in showing the gradient replacement, that is, a replacement of a diverging
term, appearing in the scaling and caused by the non-gradient nature of
the microscopic current, by a well-behaving
gradient term under a large scale space-time sample average. For this,
one needs Varadhan’s lemma, that is, the characterization of the closed forms.
So far, this method is the only mathematically rigorous way to deal with
the non-gradient models, however it is usually hard to prove. In this section,
we propose a new approach with two advantages: it avoids to show Varadhan’s lemma, and
it gives the convergence rate for the hydrodynamic limit.

As briefly explained in Section 1.1, the hydrodynamic limit was studied in

[
34
]
for the non-gradient Kawasaki dynamics on the lattice torus

\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}
. It is described as a Markov process
\eta^{N}(t)=\{\eta_{x}^{N}(t),x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}\}
on the configuration space
\mathcal{X}_{N}=\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}

governed by the infinitesimal generator

\mathcal{L}_{N}=N^{2}\mathcal{L}
, where
\mathcal{L}
is the operator defined
by (
1.8
) replacing
{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
with
\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}
.
The hydrodynamic limit is the problem to study
the asymptotic behavior as
N\to\infty
of
the macroscopic empirical mass distribution
\rho^{N}(t,dv)
of
\eta^{N}(t)

defined by (
1.10
) and the nonlinear diffusion equation
(
1.11
) was derived in the limit; see also the discussions around (
1.16
).

7.1.
Convergence rate in gradient replacement

As we pointed, one main step in non-gradient hydrodynamic limit was to show the gradient replacement.
In the previous work
[
34
]
, it is Theorem 3.2 together with Lemma 3.4. The formula in Theorem 3.2
still contains diverging factor
N
in
N^{1-d}
but it can be absorbed as in
Lemma 3.4 due to the gradient property of the function
A
defined by
(
7.3
) below.
Theorem 3.2 follows from Corollary 5.1 or especially from Theorem 5.1 for which
Varadhan’s lemma formulated in Theorem 4.1 was used.
Varadhan’s lemma roughly claims that the closed forms are determined from
and spanned by the functions
v
in the variational formula
(
1.22
) or (
1.25
) for the conductivity
\mathbf{c}(\rho)
.
Physically, the linear term appears as the first order term in the Taylor expansion
of the local equilibrium state of second order approximation (see (
7.14
) below), which corresponds to the corrector in the homogenization theory.

Let us start with a refinement of Theorem 5.1 and Corollary 5.1 in

[
34
]
. For any
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we denote by
\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}=\{0,1\}^{\Lambda}
. Using the notation (
1.44
), every configuration
\eta\in\mathcal{X}
can be decomposed into two parts

(7.1)

\displaystyle\eta=\xi\cdot\zeta,\qquad\xi:=\eta_{|\Lambda}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda},\qquad\zeta:=\eta_{|\Lambda^{c}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda^{c}}.

We let
\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda,\zeta}
be a restriction of the generator
\mathcal{L}

defined in (
1.8
) on
\Lambda
with the exterior condition

\zeta

(7.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda,\zeta}v:=\sum_{b\in\Lambda^{*}}c_{b}\pi_{b}v.

Using the decomposition
\eta=\xi\cdot\zeta
from (
7.1
) on the domain
\Lambda_{L}=(-L/2,L/2)\cap{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
, we define the quantity
A_{L},B_{L,\zeta}
and
H_{L,\zeta,F}
as

(7.3)

\begin{split}A_{L}(\xi)&:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{L}:|x-y|=1}(\xi_{y}-\xi_{x})(y-x),\\
B_{L,\zeta}(\xi)&:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x,y\in\Lambda_{L}:|x-y|=1}W_{x,y}(\eta)(y-x)=-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta}\Big(\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{L}}x\xi_{x}\Big),\\
H_{L,\zeta,F}(\xi)&:=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{L-r(F)-1}}\tau_{x}(\mathcal{L}_{N}F)(\eta)=\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta}\bigg(\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{L-r(F)-1}}\tau_{x}F\bigg)(\xi).\end{split}

Note that
W_{x,y}(\eta):=c_{x,y}(\eta)(\eta_{y}-\eta_{x})
in
B_{L,\zeta}

denotes the microscopic current, while
F=(F_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}
in
H_{L,\zeta,F}
will be taken
as the function appearing
in the local equilibrium state of second order approximation
\psi_{t}

in (
7.14
) defined in next section . The correction function
F
should be local compared
to
\Lambda_{L}
, so we require
r(F)\leqslant L-1
, where
r(F)
measures the diameter of the support of
F

(7.4)

\displaystyle r(F):=\min\big\{r\in\mathbb{N}_{+}:F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}(\Lambda_{r})\big\}.

For a function
f\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
such that
\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M}=0
, its CLT variance is defined by

\displaystyle\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}[f]

\displaystyle:=\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}[f,f],

for
0\leqslant M\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}|
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}}=\{0,1\}^{\Lambda_{L}^{c}}
, where

\displaystyle\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}[f,g]

\displaystyle:=\left\langle{f(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta})^{-1}g}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta},

Recall the expectation under the canonical ensemble
\left\langle{\cdot}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta}
defined in Section
1.4.2
. The CLT variance for the gradient replacement is defined by

(7.5)

Q_{L}(F;q,M,\zeta):=|\Lambda_{L}|^{-1}\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}\left[q\cdot\{D(M/|\Lambda_{L}|)A_{L}-(B_{L,\zeta}-H_{L,\zeta,F})\}\right]\\
-\frac{1}{2}q\cdot R(M/|\Lambda_{L}|;F)q,

where
R(\rho;F)=\mathbf{c}(\rho;F)-\mathbf{c}(\rho)
is defined in (
1.18
).

The following proposition states that, when taking
F
such that
R(\rho;F)
is very small, the non-gradient term
B_{L,\zeta}
can be replaced by a gradient term
A_{L}
plus a term
H_{L,\zeta,F}
vanishing under the time integral
and scaling.
Proposition
7.1
provides the convergence rate in the gradient replacement,
which is a quantitative refinement of the result shown in Corollary 5.1 of
[
34
]
and is a key for proving
the hydrodynamic limit in non-gradient model.

Proposition 7.1
.

There exists a positive constant
C(d,\lambda,\mathbf{r})
such that the quantity
Q_{L}
defined in (
7.5
) satisfies the following estimate for all
L\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}

(7.6)

\sup_{q,M,\zeta}|Q_{L}(F;q,M,\zeta)|\\
\leqslant C\left(L^{-\alpha_{1}}+r(F)^{d}(1+r(F)^{2d}\|F\|_{\infty}^{2})L^{-1}+r(F)^{2d}\|F\|_{\infty}L^{-d}+CL^{-1}\right).

Here the supreme is taken over all
q\in B_{1}
,
0\leqslant M\leqslant|\Lambda_{L}|
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda_{L}^{c}}
.

Proof.

We denote by
\hat{\rho}:=\frac{M}{|\Lambda_{L}|}
as the empirical density to simplify the notation. As proposed in
[
34
, Proposition 5.1 and Theorem 5.1]
, the estimate of
Q_{L}(F;q,M,\zeta)
can be reduced to the following three terms

(7.7)

\begin{split}Q_{L}^{(4)}(F;q,M,\zeta)&:=|\Lambda_{L}|^{-1}\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}\left[q\cdot(B_{L,\zeta}-H_{L,\zeta,F})\right]-\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\mathbf{c}}(\hat{\rho};F)q,\\
Q_{L}^{(5)}(F;q,\widetilde{q},M,\zeta)&:=|\Lambda_{L}|^{-1}\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}\left[\widetilde{q}\cdot A_{L},q\cdot(B_{L,\zeta}-H_{L,\zeta,F})\right]-(q\cdot\widetilde{q})\chi(\hat{\rho}),\\
Q^{(6)}_{L}(\widetilde{q},M,\zeta)&:=|\Lambda_{L}|^{-1}\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}\left[\widetilde{q}\cdot A_{L}\right]-2(\widetilde{q}\cdot{\mathbf{c}}^{-1}(\hat{\rho})\widetilde{q})\chi^{2}(\hat{\rho}).\end{split}

By choosing
\widetilde{q}=D(\hat{\rho})q
in (
7.3
), we obtain immediately an identity of
Q_{L}

(7.8)

Q_{L}(F;q,M,\zeta)\\
=Q_{L}^{(4)}(F;q,M,\zeta)-2Q_{L}^{(5)}(F;q,D(\hat{\rho})q,M,\zeta)+Q^{(6)}_{L}(D(\hat{\rho})q,M,\zeta).

To show (
7.6
), we need the error estimate for each term. Among them, the quantitative estimates of
Q_{L}^{(4)}
and
Q_{L}^{(5)}
are given in Proposition 4.1 of
[
32
]
: for
q,\widetilde{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
|q|=|\widetilde{q}|=1
,

(7.9)

\displaystyle|Q_{L}^{(4)}(F;q,M,\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leqslant Cr(F)^{d}(1+r(F)^{2d}\|F\|_{\infty}^{2})L^{-1},

(7.10)

\displaystyle|Q_{L}^{(5)}(F;q,\widetilde{q},M,\zeta)|

\displaystyle\leqslant Cr(F)^{2d}\|F\|_{\infty}L^{-d}+CL^{-1}.

The computation of
Q_{L}^{(6)}
, as the error for the CLT variance of
A_{L}
, is indeed deeply related to the dual quantity studied in (
6.14
), and this makes Varadhan’s lemma avoidable.
It is, in a sense, hidden in the variational formula for
\widehat{D}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)
.
In other words, our dual computation well fits to computing the CLT variance
of
A_{L}
. This matches with the dual computation in (5.7) in the proof of
Theorem 5.1 in
[
34
]
,
but our computation is presented at higher level on the configuration space.
We can write the error
Q_{L}^{(6)}
of the CLT variance of
A_{L}
defined by
(
7.7
) concretely in terms of the dual quantity as in (
7.11
) below.
It is important that we can give such an exact formula before taking the limit
so that one can directly apply our estimate to obtain its convergence rate.
Note that we need the uniformity in the density
\rho\in[0,1]
as in Corollary 5.1
in
[
34
]
.

Let us implement the discussion above to
Q_{L}^{(6)}
. From (
7.3
) and (
1.46
), the quantity
q\cdot A_{L}
satisfies the identity

\displaystyle q\cdot A_{L}=-\sum_{b=\{x,y\}\in(\Lambda_{L})^{*}}\pi_{b}\ell_{q}.

Then by variational formula of the dual quantity
\widehat{D}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)
defined in (
6.14
), the optimiser
u(\Lambda_{L},q)
satisfies that

\displaystyle\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{L})^{*}}c_{b}\pi_{b}u(\Lambda_{L},q)=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{L})^{*}}\pi_{b}\ell_{q}.

Recall the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta}
defined in (
7.2
), thus the quantity
(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta})^{-1}(q\cdot A_{L})
is nothing but
u(\Lambda_{L},q)
defined in (1) of Proposition
4.1

1
1

1

Here we abuse the notation because the generator defined in (
7.2
) from
[
34
]
is slightly different from (
1.23
). Nevertheless, this tiny difference does no harm; see Remark
1.5
.
(see also Remark
4.2
) because of the following identity

\displaystyle(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta})^{-1}(q\cdot A_{L})=(\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta})^{-1}\left(\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{L})^{*}}\pi_{b}\ell_{q}\right)=u(\Lambda_{L},q).

Therefore, we put this result back to
\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}[q\cdot A_{L}]

and obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}[q\cdot A_{L}]

\displaystyle=\left\langle{(q\cdot A_{L})(-\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda_{L},\zeta})^{-1}(q\cdot A_{L})}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{(q\cdot A_{L})u(\Lambda_{L},q)}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{L})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})u(\Lambda_{L},q)}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{L})^{*}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}u(\Lambda_{L},q))}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{b\in(\Lambda_{L})^{*}}\left\langle{c_{b}(\pi_{b}u(\Lambda_{L},q))^{2}}\right\rangle_{\Lambda_{L},M,\zeta}.

Here from the third line to the forth line, we use the identity
\pi_{b}\pi_{b}=-2\pi_{b}
and integration by part.
From the forth line to the fifth line, we use the variational formula of
\widehat{D}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)
once again rewritten similar to (
4.3
). This concludes that

\displaystyle|\Lambda_{L}|^{-1}\Delta_{L,M,\zeta}[q\cdot A_{L}]=\left(2\chi(\hat{\rho})\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}q\cdot\widehat{D}^{-1}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)q\right)=\chi(\hat{\rho})q\cdot\widehat{D}^{-1}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)q.

We put this result back to (
7.7
), and obtain that

(7.11)

\displaystyle Q^{(6)}_{L}(q,M,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\chi(\hat{\rho})q\cdot\left(\widehat{D}^{-1}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)-D^{-1}(\hat{\rho})\right)q

\displaystyle=\chi(\hat{\rho})q\cdot\widehat{D}^{-1}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)\left(D(\hat{\rho})-\widehat{D}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)\right)D^{-1}(\hat{\rho})q

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot\widehat{D}^{-1}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)\left(\mathbf{c}(\hat{\rho})-\hat{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)\right)D^{-1}(\hat{\rho})q.

Apply the quantitative homogenization of canonical ensemble in
(
1.27
) (see also Proposition
6.4
and
\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)=\mathbf{c}(\rho)
proved in Proposition
6.9
),
noting the uniform positivity of matrices
\widehat{D}_{*}(\Lambda_{L},M)

and
D(\hat{\rho})
in (2) of Proposition
4.1
, we obtain that

(7.12)

\displaystyle|Q^{(6)}_{L}(q,M,\zeta)|\leqslant CL^{-\alpha_{1}},

for some
C,\alpha_{1}>0
. The estimates (
7.9
), (
7.10
) and (
7.12
) together apply to (
7.8
) and yield (
7.6
).

∎

7.2.
Application to the quantitative hydrodynamic limit

The proof of the hydrodynamic limit in
[
34
]
relies on the relative entropy method
in which we compare the distribution of our system
\eta^{N}(t)
with the
local equilibrium state of second order approximation.

Let
h_{N}(f|\psi)
be the relative entropy per volume for two probability densities
f
and
\psi
with
respect to
\nu^{N}_{1/2}
defined as

(7.13)

\displaystyle h_{N}(f|\psi):=N^{-d}\int_{\mathcal{X}_{N}}f\log(f/\psi)\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu_{1/2}^{N}.

Here
\nu_{1/2}^{N}
is the Bernoulli product measure on
\mathcal{X}_{N}

with mean
1/2
. The local equilibrium state of second order approximation

\psi_{t}\equiv\psi_{t}^{N}
was defined by

(7.14)

\displaystyle\psi^{N}_{t}(\eta):=Z_{t}^{-1}\exp\bigg\{\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\lambda(t,x/N)\eta_{x}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}\partial\lambda(t,x/N)\cdot\tau_{x}F_{N}(\eta)\bigg\},

where
Z_{t}
is a normalization constant with respect to
the Bernoulli product measure
\nu^{N}_{1/2}
and
\partial\lambda\,(\equiv\nabla\lambda)=(\partial_{i}\lambda)_{i=1}^{d}
;
see p.5 of
[
34
]
or (2.1) in
[
32
]
.
Here, we determine

\lambda(t,v)
in
\psi_{t}^{N}
as
\lambda(t,v)=\overline{\lambda}(\rho(t,v))
from the solution

\rho(t,v)
of the hydrodynamic equation (
1.11
)
with
\overline{\lambda}(\rho)=\log\{\rho/(1-\rho)\}
and
F\equiv F_{N}:=\Phi_{n(N)}

with
\Phi_{n}
defined in (
6.73
) with
n=n(N)
to be determined later.

The choice of
F\equiv F_{N}:=\Phi_{n(N)}
is the main result obtained in this paper, as it gives the decay rate for
R(\rho;F)={\mathbf{c}}(\rho;F)-{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)
. Following Proposition
6.9
and Lemma
4.4
,
one can find a sequence of functions
\Phi_{n}
such that

(7.15)

\displaystyle r(\Phi_{n})\leqslant n,\qquad\|\Phi_{n}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C_{2}n^{2}\log n,\qquad\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;\Phi_{n})|\leqslant C_{2}n^{-\alpha_{2}}.

for some
C_{2},\alpha_{2}>0
independent of
\rho
.

Then, we are now ready to give the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

We consider the relative entropy

h_{N}(t):=h_{N}(f^{N}_{t}|\psi^{N}_{t})
, where we denote by
f_{t}^{N}

the density of the distribution of
\eta^{N}(t)
on
\mathcal{X}_{N}
with
respect to
\nu_{1/2}^{N}
. The proof is given by a combination of a bound for
h_{N}(t)
, the entropy
inequality and a large deviation estimate for
\psi_{t}^{N}
. Besides the previous work
[
34
]
, we also borrow some estimates from
[
32
]
, where the hydrodynamic limit for a non-gradient Glauber–Kawasaki dynamics is studied.

Step 1: relative entropy estimate.
A bound for
h_{N}(t)
was obtained for Glauber–Kawasaki dynamics with a strength
K
in the Glauber part in (3.64) of
[
32
]
.
Our Kawasaki dynamics corresponds to the case of
K=0
and, picking
the contribution from Kawasaki part only, we obtain the estimate

(7.16)

\displaystyle 0\leqslant h_{N}(t)\leqslant e^{t/\delta}\Big(h_{N}(0)

\displaystyle+Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N})

\displaystyle+C(\beta+1)\,\left\|{\partial\lambda}\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}|R(\rho;F_{N})|

\displaystyle+Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N})\Big),

for every
\beta>0
,
1\leqslant L\leqslant N/2
,
t\in[0,T]
and
\delta>0
taken sufficiently small, as in (2.6) of
[
34
]
or as
\delta_{*}>0
in Lemma 3.13 of
[
32
]
.
Note that a bound for
h(t)=\varlimsup\limits_{N\to\infty}h_{N}(t)
is given
in Theorem 2.1 in
[
34
]
, but we actually have an error term
o(1)
for
h_{N}(t)

as in Lemma 3.2 in
[
34
]
. In the above estimate (
7.16
) (which is obtained
after applying Gronwall’s inequality), we give an estimate for the term
o(1)

clarifying its decay rate.

Term 1:
Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F)
.
In (
7.16
), the first error
Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F)
for the microscopic current of non-gradient type adjusting with the corrector
F
consists of four terms as

\displaystyle Q_{N,L,\beta}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F)=\frac{C}{\beta}+\beta^{2}Q_{N,L}^{(1)}(\lambda,F)+\beta Q_{L}^{(2)}(\lambda,F)+Q_{N,L}^{(3)}(\lambda),

with
F=F_{N}
. Here, the term
Q_{N,L}^{(1)}
has a bound

(7.17)

\displaystyle|Q_{N,L}^{(1)}|\leqslant CN^{-1}L^{2d+4}(1+r(F)^{3d}\|F\|_{\infty}^{3}),

see Theorem 3.5 of
[
32
]
.
Note that
\lambda
does not depend on
K
in the present setting
so that
\|\partial\lambda\|_{\infty}
is bounded by
C
.
Note also that
\frac{C}{\beta}K
in Lemma 3.6 of
[
32
]
may be replaced by

\frac{C}{\beta}
which is needed as a cost for the entropy bound, though
we have no Glauber part.
The term
Q_{L}^{(2)}\equiv Q_{L}^{(2)}(\lambda,F)
is estimated by
[
32
, (3.31)]
as

(7.18)

\displaystyle|Q_{L}^{(2)}|\leqslant C\left\|{\partial\lambda}\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\sup_{q,M,\zeta}|Q_{L}(F;q,M,\zeta)|,

so we can cite the estimate of the gradient replacement (
7.6
) in Section 7.1 directly.

The term
Q_{N,L}^{(3)}\equiv Q_{N,L}^{(3)}(\lambda)
does not depend on
F
and
it is estimated from above as

(7.19)

\displaystyle Q_{N,L}^{(3)}\leqslant C(L^{-1}+N^{-1})+C\beta L^{-1}+\frac{C}{\beta}+C\beta^{2}N^{-1}L^{2d-2},

see Lemma 3.7 in
[
32
]
.

Term 2:
Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F)
.

The error
Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F)
appearing in a large deviation bound is estimated as

(7.20)

\displaystyle|Q_{N,L,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F)|\leqslant CN^{-1}(L+\|F\|_{\infty})+CL^{-d}\log L.

This estimate is shown in Theorem 3.9 of
[
32
]
for Glauber–Kawasaki dynamics, and for a function
G=G_{1}+\frac{1}{K}G_{2}
with
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
coming respectively from the Glauber and Kawasaki part, as in
[
32
, (3.59)]
.
In the present setting, since
G_{1}\equiv 0
in this theorem and also in Section 3.6
of
[
32
]
, one can apply it by taking
K=1
so that
G=G_{2}
. Note that
K\geqslant 1
in Theorem 3.9 of
[
32
]
was just a parameter which can be different from
K(N)
in
[
32
, (1.1)]
, and a uniform estimate in
K
was provided. Note also that,
by the same reason, we may take
K=1

in Theorem 3.11 of
[
32
]
.

Term 3:
Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F)
.

The error
Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F)
in the entropy calculation, especially in the time derivative of
h_{N}(t)
, has a bound

(7.21)

\displaystyle|Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F)|\leqslant CN^{-1}(1+r(F)^{d+2}\|F\|_{\infty})^{3},

if the condition
N^{-1}r(F)^{d}\|F\|_{\infty}\leqslant 1
is satisfied; see Lemma 3.1
in
[
32
]
.

Term 4:
Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F)
.

The error
Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F)
for the gradient term is bounded as

(7.22)

\displaystyle|Q_{N,L}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F)|\leqslant

\displaystyle C\big(N^{-1}L^{(d+2)/2}+L^{-d}(1+r(F)^{d})\big)

\displaystyle\times(1+r(F)^{2d}\|F\|_{\infty}^{2}+r(F)^{d+1}\|F\|_{\infty})+CN^{-1}.

This estimate is shown in Lemma 3.8 of
[
32
]
relying on Lemma 3.4
of
[
32
]
. To apply the latter, since
K
in the estimate covers both Glauber
and Kawasaki effects, we need to take
K=1
rather than
K=0
by a similar reason to Term 2 above.

Step 2: choice of parameters.
Now, we choose
1\ll n\ll L\ll N
and
F\equiv F_{N}=\Phi_{n(N)}
as stated at the beginning of this section, and insert the estimate (
7.15
). More precisely, we set
n,L,\beta
as mesoscopical scales

(7.23)

n:=N^{s_{1}}<L:=N^{s_{2}}<N,\quad 0<s_{1}<s_{2}<1,\qquad\beta:=n^{s_{3}},\quad s_{3}>0,

with
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}
to be determined. Then, from (
7.16
) and several estimates stated above,
we obtain for some
\mu>0

\displaystyle h_{N}(t)\leqslant C\Big(h_{N}(0)

\displaystyle+n^{-s_{3}}+n^{\mu}N^{-1}L^{2d+4}+n^{s_{3}}L^{-\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle+n^{\mu}L^{-1}+L^{-d}\log L+n^{s_{3}}n^{-\alpha_{2}}\Big).

Since
h_{N}(0)\leqslant CN^{-\alpha}
, choosing
s_{3}\in(0,\alpha_{2})
,
s_{2}=1/(2d+5)
and then

s_{1}\in(0,s_{2})
small enough, one can derive

\displaystyle h_{N}(t)\leqslant CN^{-\kappa},

for some
C,\kappa>0
.

Step 3: entropy inequality.

To show the estimate (
1.21
) in Theorem
1.3
,
consider the event

\mathcal{A}=\left\{\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N}:\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi(v)\rho^{N}(t,{\mathrm{d}}v)-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi(v)\rho(t,{\mathrm{d}}v)\right|>\varepsilon\right\}.

Then, by the large deviation estimate with respect to
P^{\psi_{t}}=\psi_{t}^{N}\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu^{N}_{1/2}
, we have

(7.24)

P^{\psi_{t}}(\mathcal{A})\leqslant e^{-C(\varepsilon)N^{d}}

for some
C(\varepsilon)>0
. Indeed, (
7.24
) is shown since the contribution
of
F_{N}
is negligible due to the factor
1/N
in (
7.14
),
the bound on
\|\Phi_{n}\|_{\infty}
and the choice of
n=n(N)
; see (2.6) and
Corollary 3.10 in
[
32
]
. Applying the entropy inequality for
P^{f_{t}}=f^{N}_{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}\nu^{N}_{1/2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle P^{f_{t}}(\mathcal{A})\leqslant\frac{\log 2+N^{d}h_{N}(f^{N}_{t}|\psi^{N}_{t})}{\log\{1+1/P^{\psi_{t}}(\mathcal{A})\}}\leqslant\frac{\log 2+CN^{d-\kappa}}{\log\{1+e^{C(\varepsilon)N^{d}}\}}\leqslant C^{\prime}N^{-\kappa}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
1.3
.
∎

Our method and estimates also apply to non-gradient Glauber–Kawasaki dynamics.
In particular, one can give the upper bound for the strength
K(N)
of the
Glauber part for which one can prove the hydrodynamic limit.
This is discussed in
[
32
]
.

8.
Extension to disordered lattice gas

We have worked with the non-gradient model where the jump rate is non-constant but a deterministic function on the configuration space. One may wonder whether the method is effective when the external randomness comes into the jump rate function. These models are known as
the disordered lattice gas
or
the exclusion process in random/inhomogeneous environment
. In the literature,
[
70
,
30
]
considered the lattice gas with disorder on site;
[
42
,
49
,
50
]
studied the cases where the jump rate depends on the random environment and combines the homogenization theory to obtain the diffusion matrix;
[
28
,
29
]
further relaxed the underlying graph to the supercritical percolation and other stationary random graphs. It is not immediate to cover the quantitative homogenization results in all these models, because there are various ways to pose the disorder and sometimes the jump rate also degenerates. Here we just give one typical example to illustrate that our proof still works in the presence of external disorder. This model can be seen as a lattice gas with disorder on bonds, where randomness is introduced without breaking the spatial homogeneous property, the uniform ellipticity and the product Bernoulli measure is still an invariant measure. The argument will give a quantitative convergence rate (with a random fluctuation) of finite-volume conductivity.

8.1.
Model and hypothesis

The notations in this new model is almost the same of the original one. However, instead of the genrator (
1.8
) defined by a collection of functions
\{c_{b}(\eta)\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}
, we now consider a collection of random functions:

\displaystyle c:\ \Omega

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}^{({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}},

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto\{c_{b}^{\omega}(\cdot)\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}},

on some probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{G},\mathbf{P})
satisfying the following hypothesis.

Hypothesis 8.1
.

The following conditions are supposed for
c:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{0}^{({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}
.

(1)

Non-degenerate and local: there exists a positive integer
\mathbf{r}
and a positive number
\lambda>1
such that for every
\omega
, the function
c^{\omega}_{x,y}(\eta)
depends only on
\{\eta_{z}:|z-x|\leqslant\mathbf{r}\}
, and is bounded on two sides
1\leqslant c^{\omega}_{x,y}(\eta)\leqslant\lambda
.

(2)

Detailed balance under Bernoulli measures: for every
\omega
, the function
c^{\omega}_{x,y}(\eta)
does not depend on
\{\eta_{x},\eta_{y}\}
.

(3)

Spatially homogeneous: the joint distribution of
(c_{x,x+e}^{\omega})_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},e\in U}
is the same as that of
(\tau_{x}c_{0,e}^{\omega})_{x\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}},e\in U}
, where both of them are viewed as a family of random functions taking value in
\mathcal{F}_{0}
.

(4)

Unit range dependence: for any two edge sets
E,F\subseteq({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}
such that if there is no adjacent pair
b\in E,\ b^{\prime}\in F
sharing a common vertex, then the functions
\{c_{b}^{\cdot}\}_{b\in E}
and
\{c_{b}^{\cdot}\}_{b\in F}
are independent.

We specially mention that given each sample
\omega\in\Omega
, the functions
\{c_{b}^{\omega}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}
satisfy the same properties as that in Hypothesis
1.1
except the spatial homogeneous property, which is replaced by the equality in distribution. Meanwhile, the detailed balance condition ensures that the product Bernoulli measure
\operatorname{Ber}(\rho)^{\otimes{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}}
is still an invariant measure for the Kawasaki dynamics of jump rate
\{c_{b}^{\omega}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}
for every
\rho\in[0,1]
.

8.2.
Quantitative stochastic homogenization

We use the renormalization approach to establish the quantitative homogenization result. Given
\omega\in\Omega
, we define the
quenched
subadditive quantities
\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p)
and
\overline{\nu}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,q)
as in (
4.1
)

(8.1)

\begin{split}\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p)&:=\inf_{v\in\ell_{p,\Lambda^{+}}+\mathcal{F}_{0}(\Lambda^{-})}\left\{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left\langle{\frac{1}{2}c^{\omega}_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right\},\\
\overline{\nu}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,q)&:=\sup_{v\in\mathcal{F}_{0}}\left\{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)|\Lambda|}\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda^{*}}}\left\langle{(\pi_{b}\ell_{q})(\pi_{b}v)-\frac{1}{2}c^{\omega}_{b}(\pi_{b}v)^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right\}.\end{split}

Then for each
\omega
, (1)-(4) in Proposition
4.1
still hold except (
4.6
) and (
4.7
). This gives the definition of the quenched diffusion matrix and conductivity

\displaystyle\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}p\cdot{\overbracket{D}}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda)p,\qquad\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda)=2\chi(\rho){\overbracket{D}}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda),

\displaystyle\overline{\nu}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}q\cdot{\overbracket{D}}_{*}^{-1}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda)q,\qquad\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda)=2\chi(\rho){\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda).

The results (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) are missing, because now for every
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and
z\in{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
we only have
\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,z+\Lambda,p)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p)
(from (3) of Hypothesis
8.1
) instead of
\overline{\nu}(\rho,z+\Lambda,p)=\overline{\nu}(\rho,\Lambda,p)
in Proposition
4.1
. Nevertheless, denoting by
\mathbf{E}
the expectation associated to
(\Omega,\mathscr{G},\mathbf{P})
, we recover

(8.2)

\begin{split}\mathbf{E}[\overline{\nu}(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1},p)]&\leqslant\mathbf{E}[\overline{\nu}(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p)],\\
\mathbf{E}[\overline{\nu}_{*}(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m+1},q)]&\leqslant\mathbf{E}[\overline{\nu}_{*}(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},q)].\end{split}

The monotone property allows us to define the limit

{\overbracket{D}}(\rho):=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathbf{E}[{\overbracket{D}}(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})],\qquad\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho):=2\chi(\rho){\overbracket{D}}(\rho).

Our goal is to prove the quantitative convergence rate of the finite-volume quenched conductivity matrix to this limit. Because
\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda)
depends on the random environment
\omega
, we need to measure the random fluctuation and we use the following notation introduced in
[
11
, Appendix A]
: for a random variable
X
in
(\Omega,\mathscr{G},\mathbf{P})
and exponents
s,\theta\in(0,\infty)
, we define

(8.3)

X\leqslant\mathcal{O}_{s}(\theta)\Longleftrightarrow\mathbf{E}[\exp((\theta^{-1}X_{+})^{s})]\leqslant 2,

where
X_{+}:=\max\{X,0\}
. Roughly, (
8.3
) tells that
X
is concentrated with a stretched exponential tail. More properties of
\mathcal{O}_{s}
can be found in
[
11
, Appendix A]
. Using this notation, we state our result as follows.

Theorem 8.2
.

Fix
t\in(0,d)
, there exists
\kappa(d,\lambda)>0
,
C(t,d,\lambda)<\infty
such that for any
\rho\in(0,1)
and any
m\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
:

(8.4)

|\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)-\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})|+|\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\rho)-\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})|\leqslant\left(C3^{-m\kappa(d-t)}+\mathcal{O}_{1}(C3^{-mt})\right).

Proof.

We follow
[
11
, Theorem 2.4]
to handle the fluctuation part. We combine the quenched subadditive quantity defined in (
8.1
) to obtain the quenched master quantity

(8.5)

\displaystyle J(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p,q):=\overline{\nu}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p)+\overline{\nu}_{*}(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,q)-p\cdot q.

Then for each
\omega
fixed, Lemmas
4.5
and
4.6
remain valid and especially we have

\displaystyle\left|\overline{\mathbf{c}}(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})-\overline{\mathbf{c}}_{*}(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\right|\leqslant C(d,\lambda)\chi(\rho)\left(\sup_{|p|=1}J(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p,{\overbracket{D}}(\rho)p)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

One can verify that
J(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p,q)
is a subadditive quantity. The key step of the proof relies on the observation in
[
11
, Lemma A.7]
, which breaks the control of subadditive quantities with mixing condition into its mean and the random fluctuation. It also applies to the mapping
\Lambda\mapsto J(\omega,\rho,\Lambda,p,q)
in our setting: for every
p\in B_{1}
, there exists a constant
C
independent of
\rho
such that

(8.6)

\displaystyle J(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m},p,{\overbracket{D}}(\rho)p)\leqslant 2\mathbf{E}[J(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p,{\overbracket{D}}(\rho)p)]+\mathcal{O}_{1}(C\lambda 3^{-(m-n)d}).

Here we have the freedom to choose the parameter
n\in(0,m)\cap\mathbb{N}_{+}
, which will determine
t
in (
8.4
) by setting
t:=d\left(1-\frac{n}{m}\right)\in(0,d)
.

Therefore, it suffices to study of the decay of the mean part
\mathbf{E}[J(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p,{\overbracket{D}}(\rho)p)]
and the proof is quite similar to what we have done in Section
5
, where the main steps are Lemmas
5.2
and
5.4
, and Proposition
5.3
. They can be carried to the disordered setting for the following reasons.

(1)

Lemma
5.2
depends on the modified Caccioppoli inequality (
2.10
), which is valid for each
\omega\in\Omega
because
\{c_{b}^{\omega}\}_{b\in({\mathbb{Z}^{d}})^{*}}
satisfies the uniform ellipticy by (1) of Hypothesis
8.1
, and the underlying invariant measure is still Bernoulli measure by (2) of Hypothesis
8.1
.

(2)

The variance decay in Lemma
5.4
uses the spatial independence, which is ensured by (3) and (4) of Hypothesis
8.1
. More precisely, let
v(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p,q)
be the optimiser of
J(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p,q)
like (
4.16
), we aim to estimate

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\mathbf{E}\left[\left\langle{\left|\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla_{x}\left(v(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p,q)-\ell_{D_{n}^{-1}q-p}\right)\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant C3^{-\beta n}+C\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}3^{-\beta(n-k)}\tau_{k},

where the quantity
D_{n}(\rho)
is defined as

\displaystyle D_{n}(\rho):=\mathbf{E}[{\overbracket{D}}_{*}(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n})^{-1}]^{-1},

and
\tau_{n}
is defined as

\tau_{n}=\sup_{p,q\in B_{1}}\mathbf{E}[J(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p,q)-J(\cdot,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n+1},p,q)].

We write
v_{n}
as a shorthand for
v(\omega,\rho,{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n},p,q)
and
v_{n-1,z}
as a shorthand of
v(\omega,\rho,z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n-1},p,q)
, then we have the decomposition

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\left|\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}\sum_{x\in{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}}\nabla_{x}\left(v_{n}-\ell_{D_{n}^{-1}q-p}\right)\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\left|\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,n-1}}\sum_{x\in z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n-1}}\nabla_{x}\left(v_{n-1,z}-\ell_{D_{n-1}^{-1}q-p}\right)\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbf{E}\left[\left\langle{\frac{1}{2\chi(\rho)}\left|\frac{1}{|{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n}|}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{n,n-1}}\sum_{x\in z+{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{n-1}}\nabla_{x}\left(v_{n-1,z}-v_{n}\right)\right|^{2}}\right\rangle_{\rho}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbf{\lambda}|D_{n}^{-1}q-D_{n-1}^{-1}q|.

The key is the cancellation in the first term under
\mathbf{E}[\left\langle{\cdots}\right\rangle_{\rho}]
. After expanding the sum, we not only use the finite-range dependence of
\eta\mapsto c^{\omega}_{b}(\eta)
to obtain the independence over
\mathbb{P}_{\rho}
, but we also use the unit-range dependence of
\mathbf{P}
.

(3)

The
L^{2}
-flatness estimate in Proposition
5.3
should also be carried under the expectation
\mathbf{E}
. It relies on the weighted multiscale Poincaré inequality (
3.15
), which does not involve the jump rate. Then we further develop it, and the variance decay in Lemma
5.4
applies to the typical case (
5.14
), which has been discussed as above. For the atypical case (
5.18
), the
L^{\infty}
estimate also applies since it only requires the log-Sobolev inequality (see the proof in Appendix
B
), which remains valid thanks to the uniform ellipticity of the jump rate by (1) of Hypothesis
8.1
.

∎

One can deduce further results built on Theorem
8.2
, and we leave them to the future work.

Appendix A
Subadditivity and Whitney inequality

A lot of results on quantitative homogenization are stated for the triadic cubes, but they actually hold for the general domain with reasonable boundary regularity, and such generalization only relies on the subadditivity and a nice decomposition. We state this observation under
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
setting, and one can adapt it easily in lattice models. In the following statement, a triadic cube
Q
in
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is an open set of type
z+(-\frac{3^{m}}{2},\frac{3^{m}}{2})^{d}
, where
z\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is called its
center
and
3^{m}
with
m\in\mathbb{Z}
is called its
size
and is denoted by
\size(Q)
. Especially, we denote by
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}=(-\frac{3^{m}}{2},\frac{3^{m}}{2})^{d}
as the cube centered at
0
and of size
3^{m}
in this section. We also denote by
|U|
the
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
-Lebesgue measure for Borel set
U
, and by
\sigma(\mathcal{C})
the area of the
(d-1)
-dimensional surface
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let the quantity
\nu
be defined on the bounded open sets of
{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
with Lipschitz boundary and satisfy the following properties.

(1)

(Spatial homogeneous) For any
z\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and open set
U\subseteq{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
,
\nu(z+U)=\nu(U)
.

(2)

(Subadditivity) Given disjoint open sets
\{U_{i}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}
such that they partition
U
in the sense that
U_{1},\cdots,U_{n}\subseteq U
and
|U\setminus(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}U_{i})|=0
, then we have

(A.1)

\displaystyle\nu(U)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{|U_{i}|}{|U|}\nu(U_{i}).

(3)

(Decay for triadic cubes) There exist two finite positive constants
C,\alpha
, such that for any triadic cube
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m}
, we have

(A.2)

\displaystyle\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{m})\leqslant C(3^{-\alpha m}\wedge 1).

Then there exists a positive constant
C^{\prime}
, such that for any open set
U
circumscribed by piecewise
C^{1}
surfaces, we have

(A.3)

\displaystyle\nu(U)\leqslant C^{\prime}\left(\frac{\sigma(\partial U)\diam(U)^{(1-\alpha)\vee 0}}{|U|}\wedge 1\right).

Remark A.2
.

As an application, for the cube
\Lambda_{L}=(-\frac{L}{2},\frac{L}{2})^{d}
with
L\geqslant 1
, we have
\nu(\Lambda_{L})\leqslant C^{\prime}L^{-(\alpha\wedge 1)}
and it gives a polynomial decay in function of the diameter.

Proof.

The case for
\diam(U)\leqslant 1
is trivial and we focus on the case that
U
of large diameter. The proof relies on the standard Whitney decomposition, which will give a family of open triadic cubes
\{Q_{j}\}_{j\geqslant 0}
of nice properties; see
[
44
, Appendix J]
for its construction and proof. In our proof, the useful properties from such decomposition are

•

\{Q_{j}\}_{j\geqslant 0}
are disjoint and they partition
U
in the sense of (2) in Lemma
A.1
;

•

\sqrt{d}\size(Q_{j})\leqslant\dist(Q_{j},\partial U)\leqslant 4\sqrt{d}\size(Q_{j})
.

Applying the subadditivity to
\{Q_{j}\}_{j\geqslant 0}
and by passing to the limit, we have

(A.4)

\displaystyle\nu(U)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{|Q_{i}|}{|U|}\nu(Q_{i}).

Let
m
be the positive integer such that

(A.5)

\displaystyle 3^{m-1}\leqslant\diam(U)\leqslant 3^{m},

then we classify the cubes by their sizes

\displaystyle I_{k}:=\{i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}:\size(Q_{i})=3^{k}\}.

Using the second properties listed above about the decomposition, all the cubes in
I_{k}
stay in distance
4\sqrt{d}3^{k}
from the boundary, then we have

(A.6)

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i\in I_{k}}Q_{i}\subseteq\{x\in U:\dist(x,\partial U)\leqslant 5\sqrt{d}3^{k}\}.

Since
U
admits piecewise
C^{1}
boundary, we denote by
\partial U=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{j}
,
where different pieces
\mathcal{C}_{j}
only have intersection of null set. Then we have the following volume estimate

(A.7)

\sum_{i\in I_{k}}|Q_{i}|\leqslant\left|\{x\in U:\dist(x,\partial U)\leqslant 5\sqrt{d}3^{k}\}\right|\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left|\{x\in U:\dist(x,\partial\mathcal{C}_{j})\leqslant 5\sqrt{d}3^{k}\}\right|\\
\leqslant 10\sqrt{d}3^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sigma(\mathcal{C}_{j})=10\sqrt{d}3^{k}\sigma(\partial U).

Then we put these estimates back to the cubes from Whitney decomposition (
A.4
) to obtain

\displaystyle\nu(U)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k=-\infty}^{0}\frac{1}{|U|}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{k}}|Q_{i}|\right)\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{k})+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{1}{|U|}\left(\sum_{i\in I_{k}}|Q_{i}|\right)\nu({\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\square$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\square$}}}_{k})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C\sigma(\partial U)}{|U|}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{0}3^{k}+\frac{C\sigma(\partial U)}{|U|}\sum_{k=1}^{m}3^{(1-\alpha)k}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{C\sigma(\partial U)}{|U|}3^{m(1-\alpha)\vee 0}.

Here apply (
A.2
) and (
A.7
) from the first line to the second line, then we use (
A.5
) to conclude the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of
L^{\infty}
norm using mixing time

In this part, we prove Lemma
4.14
. Such estimate should be generally valid for Markov chain, and we state the case of Kawasaki dynamics.

Lemma B.1
.

There exists a constant
C(\lambda,d)<\infty
, such that for any connected domain
\Lambda\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
of diameter
L
and any two functions
u,f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}u=f,

then for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
we have

(B.1)

\displaystyle\left\|{u-\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant CL^{2}\log L\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}.

Proof.

Let
P_{t}(\cdot,\cdot):\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be the transition probability of the continuous-time Kawasaki dynamics generated by the generator
\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}
, which has
\mu_{\Lambda,N}=\mathbb{P}_{\Lambda,N}
as its stationary measure for any
N\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. Then we use the Duhamel’s formula

(B.2)

\displaystyle u(\eta)-\left\langle{u}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}=\int_{0}^{\infty}(P_{t}f)(\eta)-\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

which is well-defined thanks to the spectral gap and exponential decay of the mapping
t\mapsto\mathbb{V}\!\mathrm{ar}_{\Lambda,N}[P_{t}f]
.

Then we estimate the
L^{\infty}
norm. We notice that

\displaystyle\left\|{(P_{t}f)(\eta)-\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{X}}\left(\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}P_{t}(\eta,\eta^{\prime})f(\eta^{\prime})-\sum_{\eta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\mu_{\Lambda,N}(\eta^{\prime})f(\eta^{\prime})\right)

(B.3)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{X}}\left\|{P_{t}(\eta,\cdot)-\mu_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{TV}\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}.

Here we denote by
\left\|{\cdot}\right\|_{TV}
the total variation distance and make use of its definition
[
56
, (4.7)]
. By convention in
[
56
, (4.22),(4.30),(4.31)]
, we also define

\displaystyle d(t)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{X}}\left\|{P_{t}(\eta,\cdot)-\mu_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{TV},

\displaystyle t_{\operatorname{mix}}

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:d(t)<\frac{1}{4}\right\}.

Then
t\mapsto d(t)
is decreasing and satisfies (see
[
56
, (4.33)]
)

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad d(nt_{\operatorname{mix}})\leqslant 2^{-n}.

We put this observation and (
B
) back to (
B.2
)

\displaystyle\left\|{(P_{t}f)(\eta)-\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{nt_{\operatorname{mix}}}^{(n+1)t_{\operatorname{mix}}}\left\|{(P_{t}f)(\eta)-\left\langle{f}\right\rangle_{\Lambda,N}}\right\|_{\infty}\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2d(nt_{\operatorname{mix}})\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}t_{\operatorname{mix}}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{-n}\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}t_{\operatorname{mix}}

\displaystyle=2\left\|{f}\right\|_{\infty}t_{\operatorname{mix}}.

One classical method to obtain the mixing time of Markov process is the log-Sobolev inequality; see
[
25
, (1.8)]
. In our case, we use the log-Sobolev inequality on general Kawasaki dynamics developed in
[
58
, Theorem 3]
; see also
[
61
, (2)]
and discussion there. This gives us

(B.4)

\displaystyle t_{\operatorname{mix}}\leqslant C(d,\lambda)L^{2}\log\log{L^{d}\choose N}\leqslant\widetilde{C}(d,\lambda)L^{2}\log L,

which yields (
B.1
).
∎

Remark B.2
.

The steps before (
B.4
) is standard for all the reversible Markov processes. We apply the log-Sobolev inequality for the mixing time, because the jump rate in this paper depends on the local configuration. We remark some other recent progresses
[
68
,
53
,
61
]
on the mixing time of the exclusion processes (i.e. the constant-speed Kawasaki dynamics). Their generalization on the non-gradient models is an interesting but challenging question.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

We take
u(\Lambda,q)
for example, which is the solution of

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}u(\Lambda,q)=\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda_{L}^{*}}}\pi_{b}\ell_{q}.

Therefore, we apply (
B.1
) with
\left\|{\sum_{b\in\overline{\Lambda_{L}^{*}}}\pi_{b}\ell_{q}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant C(\lambda,d)L^{d}
. The case for
v(\rho,\Lambda,\xi)
can be done similarly.
∎

Remark B.3
.

For
2\leqslant p<\infty
, we have
\|u-\langle u\rangle_{\Lambda,N}\|_{p}\leqslant CL^{2}\|f\|_{p}
without
\log L
. Indeed, we may apply Riesz–Thorin interpolation theorem for two inequalities
\|P_{t}f-\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda,N}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2\|f\|_{\infty}
and
\|P_{t}f-\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda,N}\|_{2}\leqslant e^{-cL^{-2}t}\|f\|_{2}
for some
c>0
, which follows from the spectral gap estimate for
\mathcal{L}_{\Lambda}
.
